63: 1: # Data requirements
64: 2: 1. Observations will ultimately have N=~20,000 counts, noisy and with many MAR/MNAR missing values.  
65: 3: 2. As a found
66: 4: 2. Preliminary evaluation is done using synthetic data on varying small scales. The synthetic data generation methods are developed alongside the models. Currently, the synthetic data is generated by make_synthetic_data5 (synthetic_data.py) using latents from random_points_on_hypersphere (sythetic_data.py)  
67: 5: 3. The latent is a low-dimensional manifold, with an unevenly spread prior, with high-likelihood regions surrounded by low-likelihood regions. All non-zero-likelihood regions are connected, but they might be connected by long narrow “corridors” (representing cell differentiation stages, etc). There are no inherent “classes” in the latent and it should be modeled as continuous, not categorical (any classification or clustering would be done downstream of the model). Preserving the prior structure and distribution is important for accurately representing samples.  
68: 6: 4. The main objective of the models is to infer the latent from noisy observations, while preserving the structure of the latent and avoiding mode collapse or distortion. A secondary objective might be to impute missing values (by accurately placing the incomplete sample in latent space).  
69: 7: 5. As a stepping stone, the models are used to evaluate different architectures and understand how to analyze their implicit biases, measure sample efficiency etc. Existing models are found in models.py  
70: 8: 6. The main requirements for the model are:  
71: 9:   a. Representing the prior distribution of the latent while avoiding mode collapse  
72: 10:   b. Inferring the latent from noisy and incomplete observations  
73: 11:   c. High sample efficiency to allow training on biological data with p>>N  
74: 12: 
75:

224: 123: # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
225: 124: # load_model: restore a pre-trained model from a checkpoint file.
226: 125: #
227: 126: # Expected checkpoint format (saved with torch.save):
228: 127: #   {
229: 128: #     'model_type': <key from model_factories>,

232: 131: # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

234: 133: def load_model(path, factories=model_factories, device=device):
235: 134:     """Load a pre-trained model from *path* and return it in eval mode.
236: 135: 
237: 136:     Expected checkpoint format (saved with torch.save):
238: 137:         {
239: 138:             'model_type': <key in model_factories>,
240: 139:             'state_dict': <model.state_dict()>,
241: 140:         }
242: 141: 
243: 142:     Post-load fixups applied automatically:
244: 143:       - MeansModel  : means re-fitted from data_train (the parameter has no
245: 144:                       meaning outside the dataset it was computed on).
246: 145:       - ImputationTransformer : bin_edges attached from it_bin_edges so that
247: 146:                       impute() can find them without a separate argument.
248: 147:                       (Call load_model only after it_bin_edges is defined.)
249: 148:     """
250: 149:     ckpt       = torch.load(path, map_location=device)
251: 150:     model_type = ckpt['model_type']
252: 151:     if model_type not in factories:
253: 152:         raise ValueError(f"Unknown model_type '{model_type}'. "
254: 153:                          f"Available: {list(factories)}")
255: 154:     m = factories[model_type]().to(device)
256: 155:     m.load_state_dict(ckpt['state_dict'])
257: 156:     m.eval()
258: 157:     if isinstance(m, MeansModel):
259: 158:         m.means.data = data_train.nanmean(dim=0).to(device)
260: 159:     if isinstance(m, ImputationTransformer):
261: 160:         m.bin_edges = it_bin_edges
262: 161:     return m
263: 162: 
264: 163: # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
265: 164: # Active model for the training loop below.

271: 170: if isinstance(model, MeansModel):

273: 172: learning_graph_train = []

577: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1042:Let me look at how `make_synthetic_data5` is actually being called in the codebase—particularly in vae-test.py—to see what parameters are being used for cluster weights, correlation scaling, and other configuration details. 578: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1075:18: # 2. The implicit "sample state" that allows different samples to be imputed differently  
579: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1431:Looking at the existing VAE implementations in models.py—GeneExpressionVAE, MoVEVAE, and VampPriorVAE—I can see they use different prior approaches. The user is asking whether DEQFlow from the normalizing flow demo would be a suitable choice for this hierarchical modeling problem, though the vae-test.py file itself appears to be incomplete and references undefined functions.
580: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1908:For structuring the answer, I should focus on a technical breakdown: explaining what DEQFlow is as a contraction-based implicit flow with weight-tied architecture for parameter efficiency, then addressing how it compares to other normalizing flows in terms of expressivity and how it might handle manifold-supported data.
581: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1914:The real challenge emerges with well-separated modes: a single continuous transformation from a unimodal Gaussian must essentially "tear" space to create disconnected regions, and this is where normalizing flows struggle. There's a known limitation where continuous-time flows and certain architectures (especially residual or contractive-only flows that stay close to the identity with small perturbations) have trouble producing truly isolated modes without creating low-density bridges between them—these spurious connections between modes are a classic artifact. The DEQ flow's Lipschitz constraint (coefficient 0.8) deliberately limits expressivity to guarantee invertibility, which trades off the ability to model sharp separations between modes.
582: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1918:Neither RealNVP nor DEQFlow explicitly encode anything about hierarchical gene grouping or manifold structure—they're just generic density models over the full vector space. If the problem has inherent low-dimensional manifold structure with hierarchical organization, I'd want models that actually build in that inductive bias rather than learning it from scratch.
583: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1922:Hyperspherical VAEs using von Mises-Fisher distributions on the latent space directly match the geometry of cell states lying on a hypersphere, providing much stronger inductive bias than Euclidean flows. On the practical side, DEQFlow's log probability computation requires expensive exact Jacobian determinant calculations via backward passes per block—scaling as O(N) and becoming prohibitive for high-dimensional gene expression data like the 200-gene case here, which the module documentation explicitly flags as a limitation.
584: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1924:The approach is really only feasible when applied to low-dimensional latent codes (8-32 dimensions) as a prior within a VAE framework rather than directly modeling raw gene expression. Normalizing flows also lack native mechanisms for handling missing data—unlike VAEs which can mask and impute through reconstruction likelihoods—since marginalizing a flow over arbitrary missing subsets requires the marginal density under the change-of-variables transformation, which isn't available in closed form except for special block structures. This makes flows poorly suited as direct generative models for incomplete gene expression data; they're more naturally positioned as priors over latent representations. A flow-based prior like RealNVP could handle multimodal distributions more flexibly than the current setup, though with only 4 DEQ blocks and a unimodal base, capturing very unevenly weighted or separated modes would require more computational cost. Keeping the latent dimension modest (around 8) makes this feasible while staying aligned with the intrinsic manifold structure.
585: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1958:  "command": "ls /home/michael/src/vae-test/*.png 2>/dev/null; grep -n \"DEQ\\|deq_nll\\|realnvp_nll\\|held-out\\|Held-out\" /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0948.md | head -50"
586: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1964:1:# Extending make_synthetic_data5 for DEQ VAE data generation
587: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1965:11:make_synthetic_data5 (synthetic_data.py) is used to generate gene-expression-like data that is unevenly distributed on a low-dimensional manifold. I'd like to use this data to train architectures containing a DEQ model (e.g. use a DEQ as a prior for a VAE model). The objective is to explore configurations that have a good inductive bias and are sample-efficient while preventing mode collapse, and handle noisy and incomplete data without requiring imputation of missing genes as preprocessing. What are possible approaches for extending make_synthetic_data5 to generate values for z and x?
588: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1966:3147:In the VAE + DEQ framing:

591: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1969:3198:- Natural for VampPrior / MoVE / MoE-DEQ architectures
592: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1970:3205:**Good for:** testing that DEQ-prior VAEs don't collapse modes and correctly model multi-modal distributions
593: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1971:3232:- Tests whether DEQ fixed-point iteration can capture hierarchical correlation structure
594: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1972:3234:- Natural fit for a hierarchical VAE prior or a DEQ with block-structured Jacobian
595: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1973:3241:**Good for:** benchmarking hierarchical or structured DEQ priors
596: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1974:3245:### Approach 4: Explicit `z` with a DEQ-compatible fixed-point structure
597: /home/michael/src/vae-test/session-ses_0850.md:1975:3247:Design `z` so that it is the fixed point of a map `F(z; x)`. This directly mirrors what a DEQ encoder would learn, and makes the synthetic data a natural testbed.

705: 1964: 1:# Extending make_synthetic_data5 for DEQ VAE data generation
706: 1965: 11:make_synthetic_data5 (synthetic_data.py) is used to generate gene-expression-like data that is unevenly distributed on a low-dimensional manifold. The objective is to explore configurations that have a good inductive bias and are sample-efficient while preventing mode collapse, and handle noisy and incomplete data without requiring imputation of missing genes as preprocessing. What are possible approaches for extending make_synthetic_data5 to generate values for z and x?
707: 1966: 3147:In the VAE + DEQ framing:
708: 1967: 3154:The question is: **what latent structure should `z` have, and what additional structure in `x` would make the dataset a good testbed for DEQ-based VAE priors?**
709: 1968: 3177:**Good for:** ground-truth-guided evaluation of latent recovery, testing DEQ convergence as an implicit encoder
710: 1969: 3198:- Natural for VampPrior / MoVE / MoE-DEQ architectures
711: 1970: 3205:**Good for:** testing that DEQ-prior VAEs don't collapse modes and correctly model multi-modal distributions
712: 1971: 3232:- Tests whether DEQ fixed-point iteration can capture hierarchical correlation structure
713: 1972: 3234:- Natural fit for a hierarchical VAE prior or a DEQ with block-structured Jacobian
714: 1973: 3241:**Good for:** benchmarking hierarchical or structured DEQ priors
715: 1974: 3245:### Approach 4: Explicit `z` with a DEQ-compatible fixed-point structure
716: 1975: 3247:Design `z` so that it is the fixed point of a map `F(z; x)`. This directly mirrors what a DEQ encoder would learn, and makes the synthetic data a natural testbed.
717: 1976: 3255:where `W_xx`, `W_zz`, `b` are fixed random matrices (not learned), and `x_obs` is a partially-observed version of `X`. The "true" latent is this fixed point, and the DEQ encoder's job is to recover it.
718: 1977: 3258:- The data has a ground-truth DEQ structure — the DEQ encoder is provably the right inductive bias
719: 1978: 3261:- Tests sample efficiency: fewer observations needed if the DEQ prior is correctly specified
720: 1979: 3264:- The data generation is now tied to a specific DEQ architecture, making it less general as a benchmark
721: 1980: 3268:**Good for:** a direct test of DEQ encoder correctness; less suitable for broad architecture comparison
722: 1981: 3302:For a DEQ-prior VAE testbed that is:
723: 1982: 3306:- **good inductive bias test**: `z` structure should match what the DEQ prior can capture
724: 1983: 3332:## A note on DEQ-as-prior vs. DEQ-as-encoder
725: 1984: 3334:There are two distinct roles a DEQ can play here, and they have different implications for what `z` and `x` should look like:
726: 1985: 3338:| **DEQ as encoder prior** `p(z)` | DEQ defines the prior distribution over latents; encoder `q(z|x)` is standard MLP | `z` on a structured manifold (hypersphere, hierarchical, multi-modal) |
727: 1986: 3339:| **DEQ as encoder** `q(z|x)` | DEQ iteratively refines `z` given `x` at test time; handles missing data naturally | `z` as the fixed point of `F(z; x_obs)`, where `x_obs` has NaN masked out |
728: 1987: 3340:| **DEQ as decoder** `p(x|z)` | DEQ generates `x` from `z` by fixed-point iteration | `z` low-dimensional, `x` high-dimensional with structured correlations |
729: 1988: 3342:Approach 4 is most relevant for testing the DEQ-as-encoder role. Approaches 1–3 are better for testing DEQ-as-prior.
730: 1989: 3348:2. Focus on a particular role (DEQ as encoder, prior, or decoder)?
731: 1990: 3355:Please expand on the two DEQ roles (prior vs. encoder) and their implications for z and x
732: 1991: 3365:## DEQ roles: prior vs. encoder
733: 1992: 3367:### Role 1: DEQ as a prior `p(z)`
734: 1993: 3369:The DEQ defines the **distribution over latents** before any data is seen. The prior is the fixed-point distribution of a stochastic map:
735: 1994: 3380:Prior:    z* = F(z*, ε),  ε ~ N(0, I)     [DEQ — implicit p(z)]
736: 1995: 3387:The test for whether the DEQ prior is a good inductive bias is whether its fixed-point distribution `p(z*)` matches the true latent distribution `p(z_true)`. So `z_true` (what `make_synthetic_data5` should return) needs to be on a manifold that the DEQ prior can represent well but a simple Gaussian prior cannot.
737: 1996: 3391:- Multi-modal: `cell_state` with `n_clusters > 1` — a Gaussian prior collapses modes; a DEQ prior can learn multi-modal fixed-point attractors
738: 1997: 3392:- Hierarchically correlated: the root/intermediate/leaf structure in `groups_def` — a Gaussian prior treats all latent dims independently; a DEQ prior with coupling can capture cross-dim correlations
739: 1998: 3402:**Key evaluation signal for DEQ-as-prior:**
740: 1999: 3404:After training, sample `z ~ p_DEQ(z*)` by running the DEQ to convergence on random `ε`, then check whether the distribution of `z*` samples matches the distribution of `z_true`. Metrics: MMD, Wasserstein distance on the marginal, or visual comparison via UMAP. The VAE encoder is still a standard MLP; it's the prior that's being tested.
741: 2000: 3408:### Role 2: DEQ as an encoder `q(z|x_obs)`
742: 2001: 3410:Here the DEQ defines the **inference procedure**: given a (possibly incomplete) observation `x_obs` with NaN entries, the encoder finds a latent `z` as the fixed point of:
743: 2002: 3416:The fixed-point structure is what gives this inductive bias over missing data: `G` takes both the current guess of `z` and the available observations, and iterates until consistent. Missing entries in `x_obs` don't need to be imputed before encoding — the DEQ naturally marginalizes over them because `G` operates on the observed subset at each iteration.
744: 2003: 3422:Encoder:  z* = G(z*, x_obs),  z* ~ q(z|x_obs)      [DEQ — implicit amortized inference]
745: 2004: 3428:For the encoder role, what matters is that `z_true` is recoverable from `x_obs` — the fixed-point iteration should converge to `z_true` when given the observed subset of `x`. This places constraints on the signal-to-noise ratio and the missing rate. If too many genes are missing, no encoder (DEQ or otherwise) can recover `z_true`.
746: 2005: 3431:- Low-dimensional relative to `n_genes` — so the DEQ has a compressed representation to converge to (16-d manifold in 200-d gene space is reasonable)
747: 2006: 3434:Crucially, the DEQ encoder has a specific advantage: it can iteratively refine its estimate of `z*` using progressively more consistent interpretations of the partial observation. This is analogous to message-passing inference. This advantage is most pronounced when:
748: 2007: 3435:- Missing entries are **not MCAR** but **structured** (e.g., a whole gene module is missing, which is more realistic biologically) — a DEQ can use the observed modules to refine the estimate and then infer the missing ones implicitly
749: 2008: 3436:- The observation model has **within-group correlation** (which `make_synthetic_data5` provides via `group_membership`) — the DEQ can exploit this correlation structure through iteration
750: 2009: 3442:1. **`x_latent`** `(n_cells, n_genes)`: the pre-NB linear signal `(group_membership @ z_true.T).T * influence_weights` — this is the noiseless, complete signal that a perfect encoder should recover after projecting back to `z_true`. Needed to measure whether the DEQ is converging to the right fixed point.
751: 2010: 3450:4. **Module-level missingness**: instead of random MCAR, drop entire gene groups (whole rows of `group_membership`) for some cells — tests whether the DEQ encoder can impute a missing module from correlated observed modules without any preprocessing. This is a much harder test than MCAR.
752: 2011: 3452:**Key evaluation signal for DEQ-as-encoder:**
753: 2012: 3454:Run the DEQ iteration for `k = 1, 2, 5, 10` steps and measure:
754: 2013: 3455:- How does `||z*(k) - z_true||` decrease with iteration count? A good DEQ encoder should show convergence. A standard MLP encoder has `k=1` by definition and cannot improve with more compute at test time.

2789: ## Context I gathered
2790: 
2791: I read `requirements.md`, the existing model zoo in `models.py` (`VAEBase`, `GeneExpressionVAE`, `MoVEVAE`, `VampPriorVAE`, `epoch_vae`/`masked_loss`), the working `DEQBlock`/`DEQFlow` implementation in `normalizing_flow_demo.py`, and — importantly — three prior sessions (`session-ses_0850.md`, `session-ses_0948.md`, `session-ses_09a0.md`) that already did a lot of conceptual groundwork on DEQs for this project. I want to build on that rather than repeat it.
2792: 
2793: **What's already been established (I won't re-derive this):**
2794: - `DEQFlow` (bijective, bounded-Lipschitz, bijective on raw `x`) was analyzed and judged a **poor fit**: bijections can't represent an exact lower-dimensional manifold, the contraction constraint fights uneven multimodality, it has no native missing-data handling, and its exact-Jacobian `log_det` doesn't scale past ~30 dims — never mind `n_genes=200` today or `~20,000` genes eventually.
2795: - Two more promising *roles* for a DEQ were sketched only conceptually, never implemented: **DEQ-as-encoder** (fixed-point amortized inference that naturally handles missing entries without imputation) and **DEQ-as-prior / latent energy model** (prior = distribution of fixed points of a learned map, giving a non-Gaussian, potentially multimodal, manifold-respecting prior).
2796: 
2797: ## Requirements that actually drive architecture choice
2798: 
2799: From `requirements.md`:
2800: - **(a)** Prior must represent an uneven, connected, continuous, possibly multimodal manifold — no mode collapse, no forced discreteness.
2801: - **(b)** Infer latent from noisy **and incomplete** observations (MAR/MNAR) — imputation is secondary but must fall out of the same inference.
2802: - **(c)** High sample efficiency for **p >> N** (per item 1, "N≈20,000 counts" reads as ~20,000 genes per sample — realistic human transcriptome size — while the number of cells/samples actually available for training will be much smaller). This is the sharpest constraint: any architecture whose parameter count scales with a big first linear layer (`n_genes → hidden`) is already sample-starved before a DEQ is even involved.
2803: 
2804: ## Proposed staged architecture family (simple → complex)
2805: 
2806: All variants are designed as drop-in `VAEBase` subclasses that keep the existing `encode()/decode()/forward()` 6-tuple contract, so they plug straight into `epoch_vae`, `masked_loss`, and `model_factories` in `vae-test.py` without touching the training loop.
2807: 
2808: **Stage 0 — Baseline (already exists):** `GeneExpressionVAE` / `VampPriorVAE`. Used purely as a control for comparison.
2809: 
2810: **Stage 1 — DEQ-Encoder VAE (minimal, recommended starting point).**
2811: Replace the MLP encoder's final refinement with a weight-tied fixed-point block: `z* = tanh(W_hz·h + W_zz·z* + b)` where `h = MLP(x_masked, mask)` is a small feature extractor (reusing `build_encoder`). Iterate to convergence like `DEQBlock`, but *without* needing invertibility or `log_det` (this isn't a flow — it's just an implicit-depth encoder), so it's much cheaper than `DEQFlow`. `μ, logσ²` are linear heads on `z*`. Prior stays `N(0,I)`; decoder stays a standard MLP.
2812: - **(a)** No help — Gaussian prior still risks mode collapse. This stage isolates the encoder question only.
2813: - **(b)** Direct hit: the fixed-point iteration can be driven by the *masked* input each step, so missing entries get implicitly "filled in" by consistency with observed ones — no separate imputation preprocessing, and depth is free test-time compute (run more iterations on harder/more-incomplete rows).
2814: - **(c)** Weight-tying means one shared transition function instead of N stacked layers — fewer parameters for a given effective depth, which is the standard DEQ sample-efficiency argument. But the dominant cost is still the `n_genes×2 → hidden` input layer; a DEQ alone doesn't fix that.
2815: - **Purpose:** cleanest possible test of "DEQ as encoder" in isolation, minimal risk, directly comparable to `GeneExpressionVAE` on the existing imputation-correlation sweep already in `vae-test.py`.
2816: 
2817: **Stage 2 — DEQ-Encoder + mixture prior (VampPrior or MoVE-style).**
2818: Keep Stage 1's encoder, swap the prior for the existing `VampPriorVAE` machinery (or `MoVEVAE`'s per-component heads). This directly targets requirement **(a)**, which Stage 1 explicitly doesn't address, while keeping the missing-data/sample-efficiency benefits of the DEQ encoder. Almost no new code — mostly composition of things already in `models.py`.
2819: 
2820: **Stage 3 — DEQ as an implicit (energy-based) prior over `z`, combined with a DEQ encoder.**
2821: This is the "energy-based latent variable model" framing from `session-ses_09a0.md`: prior `p(z)` is *defined* as the fixed-point distribution of a learned stochastic map `z* = F(z*, ε)`, and inference combines fixed-point attraction with a likelihood pull from `x_obs` (Langevin-style refinement, or a 2-stage encoder-then-DEQ-snap). This is the architecture most faithful to requirement (a)'s description of "high-likelihood regions surrounded by low-likelihood regions... connected by long narrow corridors" — an attractor landscape is a much more natural fit for that geometry than a mixture of Gaussians or Vamp components.
2822: - **Cons:** `p(z)` has no closed form, so ELBO's KL term can't be computed exactly — needs score matching, contrastive divergence, or a learned critic, which adds real training complexity and instability risk, and works against requirement **(c)** (sample efficiency) until it's proven to converge reliably. This is a research-grade component, not a quick add.
2823: 
2824: **Stage 4 (exploratory, tied to a different TODO in `vae-test.py`) — DEQ as decoder over gene-correlation structure.**
2825: `vae-test.py`'s own header TODOs call out modeling "the sparse but strong correlation structure between genes" explicitly. A weight-tied fixed-point decoder operating over a gene-correlation graph (rather than a flat MLP) is a natural DEQ role for that, and would feed the secondary imputation objective. Lowest priority — orthogonal to the encoder/prior questions above.
2826: 
2827: ## Where I'd start
2828: 
2829: Stage 1, then Stage 2 — they're incremental, cheap, directly testable against the existing `GeneExpressionVAE`/`VampPriorVAE` baselines and the imputation-correlation sweep already wired up in `vae-test.py`, and they isolate exactly one variable (encoder inference procedure, then prior shape) at a time rather than committing to the much riskier Stage 3 up front.

145: def _fixed_point_iterate(f, z0:torch.Tensor, max_iter:int=30, tol:float=1e-4):
146:   """Repeated substitution: iterate z <- f(z) until convergence.
147: 
148:   Minimal local copy of the same helper used by DEQBlock in
149:   normalizing_flow_demo.py (not imported from there because that module
150:   runs a full training demo as a side effect of import). Valid whenever f
151:   is a contraction (Lipschitz constant < 1), which DEQCell guarantees by
152:   construction (spectral-normalized weight * coeff < 1, composed with a
153:   1-Lipschitz tanh).
154: 
155:   Returns (z, n_iter, final_rel_change) so callers can check whether the
156:   solve actually converged within budget.
157:   """
158:   z = z0
159:   n_iter = max_iter
160:   rel_change = float("nan")
161:   for i in range(max_iter):
162:     z_next = f(z)
163:     rel_change = ((z_next - z).norm() / (z.norm() + 1e-6)).item()
164:     z = z_next
165:     if rel_change < tol:
166:       n_iter = i + 1
167:       break
168:   return z, n_iter, rel_change
169: 
170: 
171: class DEQCell(nn.Module):
172:   """
173:   Weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence: z* = coeff * tanh(W_zz @ z* + c),
174:   solved to its fixed point for a given conditioning vector c.
175: 
176:   This is the "DEQ as encoder" building block: instead of a fixed stack of
177:   N distinct MLP layers, a single shared transition is applied until it
178:   converges to self-consistency with the conditioning input c (derived
179:   from the observed/masked gene expression). This gives:
180:     - parameter efficiency: one shared (dim x dim) weight instead of N
181:       separate hidden-layer weights, relevant for the p >> N regime.
182:     - variable test-time compute: harder/more-incomplete rows can be run
183:       for more iterations without retraining or changing model capacity.
184: 
185:   Unlike DEQBlock in normalizing_flow_demo.py, this cell does not need to
186:   be invertible and never computes a Jacobian log-determinant (it isn't
187:   part of a bijective flow) -- it is only ever used as an encoder, which
188:   makes it considerably cheaper than DEQFlow's DEQBlock.
189: 
190:   Forward/backward follow the standard DEQ pattern (Bai, Kolter & Koltun,
191:   2019): solve for z* under no_grad, re-apply the transition once more
192:   with grad enabled to attach a shallow graph, then replace the
193:   backward-pass gradient with the solution of the adjoint fixed-point
194:   equation via a backward hook -- avoiding backprop through the unrolled
195:   solver entirely.
196:   """
197: 
198:   def __init__(self, dim:int, coeff:float=0.9, max_iter:int=30, tol:float=1e-4,
199:                n_power_iterations:int=5):
200:     super().__init__()
201:     self.coeff = coeff
202:     self.W_zz = nn.utils.spectral_norm(
203:         nn.Linear(dim, dim, bias=False), n_power_iterations=n_power_iterations)
204:     self.max_iter = max_iter
205:     self.tol = tol
206: 
207:     # Diagnostics populated on every forward() call, for inspection only.
208:     self.last_forward_iters:       int|None = None
209:     self.last_forward_residual:  float|None = None
210:     self.last_backward_iters:      int|None = None
211:     self.last_backward_residual: float|None = None
212: 
213:   def _f(self, z:torch.Tensor, c:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
214:     """
215:     Note: Lipshitz <= self.coeff < 1
216:     This guarantees z*=f(z*,c) has a unique solution (Banach fixed-point theorem)
217:     """
218:     return self.coeff * torch.tanh(self.W_zz(z) + c)
219: 
220:   def forward(self, c:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
221:     """c: (B, dim) conditioning vector -> z*: (B, dim) fixed point."""
222:     z0 = torch.zeros_like(c)
223:     with torch.no_grad():
224:       z_star, n_iter, residual = _fixed_point_iterate(
225:           lambda z: self._f(z, c), z0, self.max_iter, self.tol)
226:     self.last_forward_iters = n_iter
227:     self.last_forward_residual = residual
228: 
229:     z = self._f(z_star, c)  # value ~= z_star, but now differentiable
230: 
231:     if z.requires_grad:
232:       z0d = z.detach().requires_grad_()
233:       f0 = self._f(z0d, c)
234: 
235:       def backward_hook(grad):
236:         v, n_iter_bwd, residual_bwd = _fixed_point_iterate(
237:             lambda v: torch.autograd.grad(f0, z0d, grad_outputs=v, retain_graph=True)[0] + grad,
238:             grad, self.max_iter, self.tol,
239:         )
240:         self.last_backward_iters = n_iter_bwd
241:         self.last_backward_residual = residual_bwd
242:         return v
243: 
244:       z.register_hook(backward_hook)
245: 
246:     return z
247: 
248: 
249: class DEQEncoderVAE(VAEBase):
250:   """
251:   VAE with an implicit-depth (DEQ) encoder.
252: 
253:   Architecture:
254:     x_concat = [x_masked ; mask]            -- identical input every other
255:                                                 model in this file receives
256:     h = build_encoder(x_concat)             -- shared MLP feature extractor
257:     c = Linear(h)                           -- conditioning vector (latent_dim)
258:     z* = DEQCell(z -> coeff*tanh(W_zz@z + c))  -- weight-tied fixed point
259:     mu, logvar = Linear(z*), Linear(z*)
260:     z  = reparameterize(mu, logvar)
261:     recon = build_decoder(z)
262: 
263:   The prior is the standard N(0, I) and the decoder is the standard MLP
264:   decoder -- only the encoder's recognition network is replaced by a
265:   weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence instead of a fixed stack of MLP
266:   layers. This isolates the "DEQ as amortized encoder" role for direct,
267:   single-variable comparison against GeneExpressionVAE at matched
268:   encoder/decoder capacity.
269: 
270:   Note: like every other model in this file, the encoder receives
271:   randomly-filled values for missing entries (per-gene N(mean, std) noise,
272:   see get_random_mask / epoch_vae) plus a mask channel -- this class does
273:   not by itself remove that preprocessing step. What it adds is a
274:   weight-tied, variable-depth refinement of the latent given that input,
275:   with convergence diagnostics
276:   available via self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters / last_forward_residual.
277:   """
278: 
279:   def __init__(self,
280:                n_genes:int,
281:                latent_dim:int = 8,
282:                hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
283:                encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
284:                decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
285:                dropout:float = 0.05,
286:                coeff:float = 0.9,
287:                max_iter:int = 30,
288:                tol:float = 1e-4):
289: 
290:     super().__init__(n_genes,
291:                       latent_dim,
292:                       hidden_dims,
293:                       encoder_dims,
294:                       decoder_dims,
295:                       dropout)
296: 
297:     # Feature extractor (same shape convention as build_encoder elsewhere)
298:     self.encoder = build_encoder(n_genes, encoder_dims, dropout)
299:     self.fc_cond = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
300: 
301:     # Weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence
302:     self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff=coeff, max_iter=max_iter, tol=tol)
303: 
304:     # Latent heads (applied to the DEQ fixed point, not to h directly)
305:     self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
306:     self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
307: 
308:     # Build decoder
309:     self.decoder = build_decoder(n_genes, latent_dim, decoder_dims, dropout)
310: 
311:   def encode(self, x:torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
312:     h = self.encoder(x)
313:     c = self.fc_cond(h)
314:     z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
315:     return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)
316: 
317:   def decode(self, z:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
318:     return self.decoder(z)
319: 
320:   def forward(self, x:torch.Tensor):
321:     _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
322:     z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
323:     recon = self.decode(z)
324:     return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
325: 
326: 
327: class MoVEVAE(VAEBase):
328:   """
329:   Mixture of Variational Encoders using GeneExpressionVAE decoders.
330: 
331:   Architecture:
332:     - Shared encoder backbone (from VAEBase)
333:     - Per-component μ/logvar heads (component-specific recognition)
334:     - Per-component decoders (component-specific generative model)
335:     - Gating network for component selection
336:   """
337:   def __init__(self,
338:                n_genes:int,
339:                latent_dim:int=8,
340:                n_components:int=3,
341:                gating_net_dim:int=32,
342:                hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
343:                encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
344:                decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
345:                dropout:float = 0.05):
346: 
347:     super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, hidden_dims, encoder_dims, decoder_dims, dropout)
348: 
349:     self.n_components = n_components
350: 
351:     #self.component_encoders = nn.ModuleList([
352:     #    nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim) for _ in range(n_components)
353:     #])
354:     #self.component_encoders_logvar = nn.ModuleList([
355:     #    nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim) for _ in range(n_components)
356:     #])
357: 
358:     # Build encoder
359:     self.encoder = build_encoder(n_genes, encoder_dims, dropout)
360: 
361:     # Build latent
362:     self.fc_mu     = nn.ModuleList([
363:         nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim) for _ in range(n_components)
364:     ])
365:     self.fc_logvar = nn.ModuleList([
366:         nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim) for _ in range(n_components)
367:     ])
368: 
369:     # Build gating network
370:     self.gating = nn.Sequential(
371:         nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, gating_net_dim),
372:         nn.ReLU(),
373:         nn.Linear(gating_net_dim,    n_components)
374:     )
375: 
376:     # Build decoders
377:     self.decoders = nn.ModuleList([
378:         build_decoder(n_genes, latent_dim, decoder_dims, dropout)
379:         for _ in range(n_components)
380:     ])

435: def gaussian_log_prob(z: torch.Tensor, mu: torch.Tensor, logvar: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
436:     """
437:     Element-wise log N(z; mu, exp(logvar)).
438:     Supports broadcasting: z/mu/logvar can be (..., D).
439:     Returns a tensor of the same shape as inputs (before any .sum()).
440:     """
441:     return -0.5 * (math.log(2 * math.pi) + logvar + (z - mu).pow(2) / logvar.exp())
442: 
443: class VampPriorVAE(VAEBase):
444:   """
445:   VAE with Variational Mixture of Posteriors (VampPrior).
446: 
447:   The prior p(z) = (1/K) sum_k q_phi(z | u_k) is a mixture of K encoder
448:   posteriors evaluated at K learnable pseudo-inputs u_k (in gene-expression
449:   space).  The KL divergence KL(q(z|x) || p(z)) is estimated with a single
450:   Monte-Carlo sample z ~ q(z|x) using log-sum-exp over the K components.
451: 
452:   Architecture is identical to GeneExpressionVAE (single encoder head, single
453:   decoder); all expressiveness comes from the flexible prior.
454: 
455:   forward() returns the same 6-tuple as MoVEVAE so it is a drop-in replacement
456:   in epoch_vae.  The VampPrior KL scalar is stored in self._vamp_kl after each
457:   forward pass for use by the training loop.
458:   """
459: 
460:   def __init__(self,
461:                n_genes:      int,
462:                latent_dim:   int = 8,
463:                n_pseudo:     int = 20,
464:                hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
465:                encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
466:                decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
467:                dropout:      float = 0.05,
468:                pseudo_init_samples: torch.Tensor|None = None):
469: 
470:     super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, hidden_dims, encoder_dims, decoder_dims, dropout)
471: 
472:     self.n_pseudo = n_pseudo
473: 
474:     # Encoder (single head — same as GeneExpressionVAE)
475:     self.encoder   = build_encoder(n_genes, self.encoder_dims, dropout)
476:     self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
477:     self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
478: 
479:     # Decoder
480:     self.decoder = build_decoder(n_genes, latent_dim, self.decoder_dims, dropout)
481: 
482:     # Learnable pseudo-inputs in gene-expression space (K, n_genes).
483:     # A zero mask (all-observed) is appended before encoding so the
484:     # encoder sees a valid augmented input.
485:     if pseudo_init_samples is None:
486:       self.pseudo_inputs = nn.Parameter( torch.randn(n_pseudo, n_genes) * 0.01 )
487:     else:
488:       sample = torch.randint( 0, pseudo_init_samples.shape[0], size=(n_pseudo,) )
489:       self.pseudo_inputs = nn.Parameter( pseudo_init_samples[sample] )
490:     assert(self.pseudo_inputs.data.shape==(n_pseudo, n_genes))
491: 
492:     # Populated by forward(); read by epoch_vae.
493:     self._vamp_kl: torch.Tensor|None = None
494: 
495:   def _pseudo_encoded(self) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
496:     """Encode all K pseudo-inputs; return (pu_mu, pu_logvar) each (K, D)."""
497:     zero_mask  = torch.zeros(self.n_pseudo, self.n_genes, device=self.pseudo_inputs.device)
498:     pseudo_aug = torch.cat([self.pseudo_inputs, zero_mask], dim=1)  # (K, n_genes*2)
499:     h = self.encoder(pseudo_aug)
500:     return self.fc_mu(h), self.fc_logvar(h)  # (K, D), (K, D)
501: 
502:   def encode(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
503:     h = self.encoder(x)
504:     return h, self.fc_mu(h), self.fc_logvar(h)
505: 
506:   def decode(self, z: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
507:     return self.decoder(z)
508: 
509:   def vampprior_kl(self,
510:                    mu:     torch.Tensor,
511:                    logvar: torch.Tensor,
512:                    z:      torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
513:     """
514:     KL( q(z|x) || p_vamp(z) ) estimated with a single sample z ~ q(z|x).
515: 
516:     KL = E_q[log q(z|x)] - E_q[log p(z)]
517:        = E_q[log q(z|x)] - E_q[log (1/K) sum_k q(z|u_k)]
518: 
519:     mu, logvar, z : (B, D)
520:     returns scalar
521:     """
522:     # log q(z | x) : (B,)
523:     log_q = gaussian_log_prob(z, mu, logvar).sum(dim=-1)
524: 
525:     # Encode all pseudo-inputs once per forward pass
526:     pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._pseudo_encoded()  # (K, D)
527: 
528:     # Broadcast: z (B,1,D), pu_mu/pu_logvar (1,K,D)
529:     log_p_k = gaussian_log_prob(
530:         z.unsqueeze(1),           # (B, 1, D)
531:         pu_mu.unsqueeze(0),       # (1, K, D)
532:         pu_logvar.unsqueeze(0),   # (1, K, D)
533:     ).sum(dim=-1)                 # (B, K)
534: 
535:     # effective K (diagnostic)
536:     # range: 1.0 - n_pseudo
537:     with torch.no_grad():
538:       log_resp = log_p_k - torch.logsumexp( log_p_k, dim=1, keepdim=True ) # (B, K)
539:       resp = log_resp.exp()
540:       # effective K = exp(mean_batch[ entropy(resp)])
541:       ent = -(resp * (resp + 1e-20).log()).sum(dim=1)  # (B,)
542:       self._effective_K = torch.exp(ent.mean())
543: 
544:     log_p = torch.logsumexp(log_p_k, dim=1) - math.log(self.n_pseudo)  # (B,)
545: 
546:     return (log_q - log_p).mean()  # scalar
547: 
548:   def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
549:     """
550:     Returns: (recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None)
551:     Side-effect: stores VampPrior KL in self._vamp_kl after each call.
552:     """
553:     _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
554:     z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
555:     recon = self.decode(z)
556:     self._vamp_kl = self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)
557:     return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
558:

643: # SSM Beta Controller, World, Controller, and REINFORCE training loop

646: class RunningNorm:
647:     """
648:     Per-episode exponential moving-average normalizer.
649: 
650:     Tracks mean and variance of a D-dimensional signal using EMA.
651:     Call .update(x) each step, then .normalize(x) to z-score.
652:     Reset with .reset() at the start of each episode.
653:     """
654:     def __init__(self, dim: int, alpha: float = 0.1, eps: float = 1e-6):
655:         self.dim   = dim
656:         self.alpha = alpha
657:         self.eps   = eps
658:         self.reset()
659: 
660:     def reset(self):
661:         self.mean = torch.zeros(self.dim)
662:         self.var  = torch.ones(self.dim)
663:         self._initialized = False
664: 
665:     def update(self, x: torch.Tensor):
666:         """x: (dim,) — one observation vector (detached, on CPU)."""
667:         x = x.detach().cpu().float()
668:         if not self._initialized:
669:             self.mean = x.clone()
670:             self.var  = torch.ones(self.dim)
671:             self._initialized = True
672:         else:
673:             self.mean = (1 - self.alpha) * self.mean + self.alpha * x
674:             self.var  = (1 - self.alpha) * self.var  + self.alpha * (x - self.mean).pow(2)
675: 
676:     def normalize(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
677:         """Returns z-scored x using current EMA statistics."""
678:         mean = self.mean.to(x.device)
679:         std  = self.var.clamp(min=self.eps).sqrt().to(x.device)
680:         return (x - mean) / std
681:

## Assistant (Plan · Claude Sonnet 5 · 1651.0s)

_Thinking:_

Now I'm ready to explain the Stage 2 structure to the user. I can present the resulting model architecture clearly, laying out the two main implementation paths: either a single-head approach with a Vamp-style prior, or a mixture-of-experts setup where each component gets its own head, similar to MoVE.

## Stage 2 in `deq_dev_plan.md`

The plan (models.py:2817-2818) describes Stage 2 as:

> Keep Stage 1's encoder, swap the prior for the existing `VampPriorVAE` machinery (or `MoVEVAE`'s per-component heads)... Almost no new code — mostly composition of things already in `models.py`.

Stage 1 (`DEQEncoderVAE`, already implemented at models.py:249-324) only replaces the *encoder* (weight-tied `DEQCell` fixed-point instead of a fixed-depth MLP), keeping a plain `N(0,I)` prior. Stage 2 targets requirement (a) — the multimodal/manifold prior — by replacing that `N(0,I)` prior while keeping the DEQ encoder. The plan names two ways to do this, and doesn't pick one:

**Option A — VampPrior-style (most direct composition, single encoder head)**

```
x_concat = [x_masked ; mask]
h        = build_encoder(x_concat)             # same as DEQEncoderVAE
c        = Linear(h)                            # conditioning vector
z*       = DEQCell(z -> coeff*tanh(W_zz@z + c)) # Stage 1's fixed-point encoder
mu, logvar = Linear(z*), Linear(z*)
z        = reparameterize(mu, logvar)
recon    = build_decoder(z)

# VampPrior addition (from VampPriorVAE):
pseudo_inputs (K, n_genes)                      # learnable, K≈50
pu_mu, pu_logvar = encode(pseudo_inputs)         # same encoder+DEQ path, reused
p(z) = (1/K) Σ_k q(z | u_k)                      # mixture of encoder posteriors
_vamp_kl = KL(q(z|x) || p_vamp(z))               # replaces analytic N(0,I) KL in epoch_vae
```

This is nearly a straight subclass merge: take `VampPriorVAE`'s `pseudo_inputs` / `_pseudo_encoded` / `vampprior_kl` machinery and graft it onto `DEQEncoderVAE`'s `encode()` (so pseudo-inputs get pushed through the same encoder→`DEQCell` path as real data). `epoch_vae` already has the hook it needs (`kl_override = model._vamp_kl if isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)` at models.py:600) — that check would need to be loosened (e.g. to a duck-typed `hasattr(model, '_vamp_kl')`) or the new class would need to subclass `VampPriorVAE` for the isinstance check to fire.

**Option B — MoVE-style (mixture of experts, per-component heads/decoders)**

```
h = build_encoder(x_concat)
c = Linear(h)  per component k=1..K            # K separate conditioning vectors
z*_k = DEQCell_k(...)   or one shared DEQCell reused per component
mu_k, logvar_k = Linear(z*_k), Linear(z*_k)     # K heads, like MoVEVAE.fc_mu ModuleList
gate = softmax(Linear(h))                       # component selection, like MoVEVAE.gating
recon = Σ_k gate_k * decoder_k(z_k)              # per-component decoders
```

This is a bigger change: it requires either K independent `DEQCell`s (more parameters, cuts against the "p >> N" sample-efficiency argument that motivated the DEQ encoder in the first place) or one shared `DEQCell` conditioned on a per-component vector `c_k` — the latter is more in the spirit of Stage 1 but isn't literally "MoVEVAE's per-component heads," it's a hybrid.

### Where this leaves things

The plan itself stops short of choosing between A and B or writing the class. Given the stated rationale for Stage 1 (parameter efficiency via weight-tying, which Option B partially undermines by multiplying `DEQCell`s), Option A composes more cleanly with what's already built and keeps the single-variable-at-a-time comparison discipline the plan argues for (Stage 1 isolated encoder, Stage 2 isolates prior shape only).

_Thinking:_

Good — here's the concrete structure for **Option A**, composing Stage 1's DEQ encoder with `VampPriorVAE`'s mixture-of-posteriors prior.

## Proposed class: `DEQVampVAE` (or `DEQEncoderVampVAE`)

Subclasses `VampPriorVAE` (not `DEQEncoderVAE`) so the existing `isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)` check in `epoch_vae` (models.py:600) continues to work unchanged — only `encode()` is overridden to route through the DEQ cell instead of a plain MLP head.

```
__init__(n_genes, latent_dim=32, n_pseudo=50,
         encoder_dims=[512,256,128], decoder_dims=[128,256,256], dropout=0.02,
         coeff=0.9, max_iter=30, tol=1e-4,
         pseudo_init_samples=None):

    super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, ..., n_pseudo, pseudo_init_samples)
    # VampPriorVAE.__init__ already builds:
    #   self.encoder, self.fc_mu, self.fc_logvar, self.decoder, self.pseudo_inputs

    # Stage 1 additions, grafted on top:
    self.fc_cond  = Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim)        # conditioning vector
    self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff, max_iter, tol)
    # fc_mu / fc_logvar (inherited) now applied to z* instead of h directly —
    # or re-declared as Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim) since DEQCell output
    # dim == latent_dim, not enc_out_dim.

encode(x):
    h = self.encoder(x)              # unchanged from VampPriorVAE
    c = self.fc_cond(h)
    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
    return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)

_pseudo_encoded():                    # unchanged signature, but now goes through DEQ too
    pseudo_aug = cat(pseudo_inputs, zero_mask)
    h = self.encoder(pseudo_aug)
    c = self.fc_cond(h)
    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)          # same fixed-point cell, reused for pseudo-inputs
    return self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)

forward(x):                           # identical to VampPriorVAE.forward
    _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
    z = reparameterize(mu, logvar)
    recon = self.decode(z)
    self._vamp_kl = self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)
    return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
```

### Key design point: pseudo-inputs share the DEQ cell

The `n_pseudo` learnable pseudo-inputs get encoded through the *same* `DEQCell` as real data (just like they already go through the same `encoder`/`fc_mu`/`fc_logvar` in `VampPriorVAE`). This means the prior's mixture components `q(z|u_k)` are themselves fixed points of the shared weight-tied recurrence — the prior and the posterior are defined by the same implicit function, evaluated at different conditioning vectors. This is a nice conceptual property: no separate "prior network," the mixture is just the DEQ encoder's response to K learned inputs.

One wrinkle worth flagging: `DEQCell.forward()` records diagnostics (`last_forward_iters`/`last_forward_residual`) as instance attributes on every call — calling it twice per training step (once for the real batch in `encode()`, once for pseudo-inputs in `_pseudo_encoded()`) means the diagnostics after a `forward()` call reflect only the pseudo-input solve, clobbering the real-batch numbers. Minor fix needed (e.g. separate diagnostic fields, or read them for real-batch immediately after that specific call) if you want to keep monitoring convergence-vs-mask-fraction as Stage 1 does.

### Integration
- New class in `models.py`, next to `VampPriorVAE` (or right after `DEQEncoderVAE`).
- New factory `_make_deq_vamp_vae(n_genes)`, matching capacity to `_make_deq_encoder_vae` (`encoder_dims=[512,256,128]`, `latent_dim=32`, `coeff=0.9`) plus `n_pseudo=50` from `_make_vamp_vae`.
- Register as `model_factories['DEQVampVAE']`.
- No changes needed to `epoch_vae` — `isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)` already picks up the KL override since we subclass it.

### What this tests (per the plan's evaluation framing)
Directly comparable to both `DEQEncoderVAE` (isolates: did adding the Vamp prior help mode-coverage/requirement (a)?) and `VampPriorVAE` (isolates: did swapping in the DEQ encoder help missing-data/sample-efficiency, requirement (b)/(c), on top of the mixture prior?) — a 2×2 essentially falls out: `{MLP, DEQ} encoder × {Gaussian, Vamp} prior}`, using baselines already in `model_factories`.

## Assistant (Plan · Claude Sonnet 5 · 35.5s)

_Thinking:_

I'm laying out the exact code changes needed: a new DEQVampVAE class positioned right after VampPriorVAE, along with its factory function and registration. I also need to tackle the diagnostics issue by proposing a solution like separate diagnostic attributes or capturing values immediately after the main encode call. I'll present the specific code with line numbers and insertion points for review.

The core problem is that DEQCell stores iteration and residual diagnostics as instance attributes, so when vampprior_kl calls the pseudo-input encode later in the forward pass, it overwrites those diagnostics with pseudo-input stats instead of preserving the real batch metrics. I need to capture the real batch diagnostics right after the main encode completes, before they get clobbered. I'll go ahead and write out the full plan with code examples so we can review it before moving forward with the implementation.

Here's the exact code I'd add/change, for your review before implementing.

## 1. New class `DEQVampVAE` — insert after `VampPriorVAE` (after models.py:559, before `epoch_vae`)

```python
class DEQVampVAE(VampPriorVAE):
  """
  Stage 2: VampPriorVAE with a DEQ (implicit-depth) encoder instead of a
  plain MLP head.

  Combines two previously-isolated changes:
    - DEQEncoderVAE's weight-tied fixed-point recognition network (encoder
      question / requirements b,c)
    - VampPriorVAE's mixture-of-posteriors prior (prior question /
      requirement a)

  Both the real batch and the K learnable pseudo-inputs are encoded through
  the *same* DEQCell -- the prior's mixture components q(z|u_k) are fixed
  points of the identical implicit function that defines the posterior,
  just evaluated at different conditioning vectors. There is no separate
  "prior network."

  Subclasses VampPriorVAE (rather than DEQEncoderVAE) so the existing
  `isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)` check in epoch_vae picks up the
  VampPrior KL override with no changes to the training loop.
  """

  def __init__(self,
               n_genes:      int,
               latent_dim:   int = 32,
               n_pseudo:     int = 50,
               hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
               encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
               decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
               dropout:      float = 0.02,
               coeff:        float = 0.9,
               max_iter:     int = 30,
               tol:          float = 1e-4,
               pseudo_init_samples: torch.Tensor|None = None):

    super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, n_pseudo,
                      hidden_dims, encoder_dims, decoder_dims, dropout,
                      pseudo_init_samples)
    # VampPriorVAE.__init__ already built:
    #   self.encoder, self.decoder, self.pseudo_inputs
    #   self.fc_mu / self.fc_logvar as Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
    # Re-point fc_mu/fc_logvar to take the DEQ fixed point (latent_dim),
    # not the raw encoder features (enc_out_dim), as input.
    self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
    self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)

    self.fc_cond  = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
    self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff=coeff, max_iter=max_iter, tol=tol)

    # Diagnostics for the *real batch* solve, captured before the
    # pseudo-input solve (triggered by vampprior_kl) overwrites
    # self.deq_cell's shared last_forward_* attributes.
    self.last_batch_iters:    int|None = None
    self.last_batch_residual: float|None = None

  def _encode_via_deq(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
    h = self.encoder(x)
    c = self.fc_cond(h)
    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
    return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)

  def encode(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
    return self._encode_via_deq(x)

  def _pseudo_encoded(self) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
    """Encode all K pseudo-inputs through the same encoder+DEQ path."""
    zero_mask  = torch.zeros(self.n_pseudo, self.n_genes, device=self.pseudo_inputs.device)
    pseudo_aug = torch.cat([self.pseudo_inputs, zero_mask], dim=1)
    _, pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._encode_via_deq(pseudo_aug)
    return pu_mu, pu_logvar

  def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
    """
    Returns: (recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None)
    Side-effect: stores VampPrior KL in self._vamp_kl (read by epoch_vae),
    and captures real-batch DEQ convergence diagnostics in
    self.last_batch_iters/residual before the pseudo-input pass overwrites
    self.deq_cell's shared attributes.
    """
    _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
    self.last_batch_iters    = self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters
    self.last_batch_residual = self.deq_cell.last_forward_residual

    z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
    recon = self.decode(z)
    self._vamp_kl = self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)  # this re-runs deq_cell on pseudo-inputs
    return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
```

Notes on this design:
- `vampprior_kl()` is inherited unchanged from `VampPriorVAE` — it just calls `self._pseudo_encoded()`, which we've overridden.
- `decode()` is inherited unchanged.
- Total new code: ~45 lines, zero changes to `VampPriorVAE`, `DEQCell`, or `epoch_vae`.

## 2. Factory — insert after `_make_vamp_vae` (models.py:2139)

```python
def _make_deq_vamp_vae(n_genes:int):
    # Matches _make_deq_encoder_vae's encoder/decoder/latent capacity so
    # DEQEncoderVAE vs DEQVampVAE isolates only the prior (Gaussian vs Vamp);
    # matches _make_vamp_vae's n_pseudo so VampPriorVAE vs DEQVampVAE
    # isolates only the encoder (MLP vs DEQ).
    return DEQVampVAE(
        n_genes=n_genes,
        dropout=0.02,
        encoder_dims=[512, 256, 128],
        decoder_dims=[128, 256, 256],
        latent_dim=32,
        n_pseudo=50,
        coeff=0.9,
        max_iter=30,
        tol=1e-4,
        pseudo_init_samples=None)
```

    'MoVEVAE_K3':           _make_move_vae_k3,
    'MoVEVAE_K1':           _make_move_vae_k1,

## Open questions before I implement

1. **Diagnostics fix** — I've chosen the minimal-diff approach (capture `last_batch_iters/residual` into new attributes right after the real-batch `encode()` call, before `vampprior_kl()`'s pseudo-input pass overwrites `DEQCell`'s shared fields). An alternative would be modifying `DEQCell.forward()` itself to accept a caller-supplied dict/tag to write diagnostics into, which is more general but touches the class Stage 1 already validated. I'd lean toward the local capture (no changes to `DEQCell`) — agree?

2. **fc_mu/fc_logvar re-declaration** — since `VampPriorVAE.__init__` already creates `self.fc_mu`/`self.fc_logvar` as `Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim)`, and we need `Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)` instead (input is the DEQ fixed point, not raw encoder features), I overwrite them post-`super().__init__()`. This leaves two unused `Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim)` modules allocated then discarded — harmless (no dead params saved in state_dict since they're rebound before any forward pass, but they do exist briefly during `__init__`). Fine as-is, or would you rather avoid the wasted allocation by restructuring `VampPriorVAE.__init__` to make head construction overridable?

3. **Naming** — `DEQVampVAE` vs `DEQEncoderVampVAE` (more consistent with `DEQEncoderVAE`'s naming, but longer)?

Here, mu/logvar must instead be read off the DEQ fixed point\n    z* (dim == latent_dim), so those two Linear layers are immediately\n    overwritten with Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim) versions after\n    super().__init__() returns. The two original Linear(enc_out_dim,\n    latent_dim) modules are allocated and then discarded (replaced before\n    any forward/optimizer step touches them) -- wasted construction cost\n    only, no effect on parameters actually trained or saved.\n\n  Subtlety #4 -- DEQCell diagnostics get overwritten by the pseudo-input pass:\n    DEQCell.forward() records convergence diagnostics (last_forward_iters /\n    last_forward_residual) as attributes on the DEQCell instance itself,\n    updated on every call. None until the first forward() call.\n    self.last_batch_iters:    int|None = None\n    self.last_batch_residual: float|None = None\n\n  def _encode_via_deq(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n    \"\"\"Shared path for both real data and pseudo-inputs (see Subtlety #1).\"\"\"\n    h = self.encoder(x)\n    c = self.fc_cond(h)\n    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)\n    return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)\n\n  def encode(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n    return self._encode_via_deq(x)\n\n  def _pseudo_encoded(self) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Encode all K pseudo-inputs through the same encoder+DEQ path as real\n    data (overrides VampPriorVAE._pseudo_encoded, which used a plain MLP\n    head). }

+class DEQEncoderVampVAE(VampPriorVAE):
+  """
+  Stage 2: VampPriorVAE with a DEQ (implicit-depth) encoder instead of a
+  plain MLP head.
+
+  Combines two previously-isolated changes:
+    - DEQEncoderVAE's weight-tied fixed-point recognition network (encoder
+      question / requirements b,c: missing-data handling, sample efficiency)
+    - VampPriorVAE's mixture-of-posteriors prior (prior question /
+      requirement a: multimodal, mode-collapse-resistant prior)
+
+  Subtlety #1 -- prior and posterior share one DEQCell:
+    Both the real batch and the K learnable pseudo-inputs are encoded
+    through the *same* DEQCell instance. This means the prior's mixture
+    components q(z|u_k) are fixed points of the identical implicit function
+    that defines the posterior q(z|x), just evaluated at different
+    conditioning vectors -- there is no separate "prior network" the way
+    there would be for, e.g., a learned normalizing-flow prior. This is a
+    deliberate design choice (keeps the "one weight-tied transition" DEQ
+    argument intact) but it does mean the two roles are coupled: any change
+    to W_zz that helps fit real data also reshapes every pseudo-input's
+    fixed point, and vice versa.
+
+  Subtlety #2 -- subclassing VampPriorVAE, not DEQEncoderVAE:
+    epoch_vae (models.py) dispatches the VampPrior KL override via
+    `isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)`. Subclassing VampPriorVAE means that
+    check fires with zero changes to the shared training loop. The
+    DEQ-specific pieces (fc_cond, deq_cell) are grafted on top instead of
+    inherited from DEQEncoderVAE, since VampPriorVAE.__init__ already builds
+    everything else this class needs (encoder, decoder, pseudo_inputs,
+    vampprior_kl).
+
+  Subtlety #3 -- fc_mu/fc_logvar are re-declared, not reused:
+    VampPriorVAE.__init__ (via super().__init__ below) allocates
+    self.fc_mu / self.fc_logvar as Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim), sized to
+    consume the *raw encoder output* h directly (its own encode() applies
+    them to h). Here, mu/logvar must instead be read off the DEQ fixed point
+    z* (dim == latent_dim), so those two Linear layers are immediately
+    overwritten with Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim) versions after
+    super().__init__() returns. The two original Linear(enc_out_dim,
+    latent_dim) modules are allocated and then discarded (replaced before
+    any forward/optimizer step touches them) -- wasted construction cost
+    only, no effect on parameters actually trained or saved.
+
+  Subtlety #4 -- DEQCell diagnostics get overwritten by the pseudo-input pass:
+    DEQCell.forward() records convergence diagnostics (last_forward_iters /
+    last_forward_residual) as attributes on the DEQCell instance itself,
+    updated on every call. Within one forward() call here, deq_cell runs
+    twice: once for the real batch (inside encode()), and once more for the
+    K pseudo-inputs (inside vampprior_kl() -> _pseudo_encoded(), triggered
+    right after). The second call silently overwrites the first call's
+    diagnostics, so reading self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters after forward()
+    returns would reflect the pseudo-input solve, not the real-batch solve
+    we actually want to track (e.g. iteration count vs. mask fraction, the
+    diagnostic DEQEncoderVAE was built to expose). Fixed by snapshotting
+    self.last_batch_iters/residual immediately after the real-batch encode()
+    call, before vampprior_kl() runs and clobbers deq_cell's shared state.
+  """
+
+  def __init__(self,
+               n_genes:      int,
+               latent_dim:   int = 32,
+               n_pseudo:     int = 50,
+               hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
+               encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
+               decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
+               dropout:      float = 0.02,
+               coeff:        float = 0.9,
+               max_iter:     int = 30,
+               tol:          float = 1e-4,
+               pseudo_init_samples: torch.Tensor|None = None):
+
+    super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, n_pseudo,
+                      hidden_dims, encoder_dims, decoder_dims, dropout,
+                      pseudo_init_samples)
+    # VampPriorVAE.__init__ already built self.encoder, self.decoder,
+    # self.pseudo_inputs, and (see Subtlety #3) fc_mu/fc_logvar sized for
+    # the *wrong* input dim -- replace them here.
+    self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
+    self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
+
+    self.fc_cond  = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
+    self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff=coeff, max_iter=max_iter, tol=tol)
+
+    # Real-batch DEQ convergence diagnostics, snapshotted in forward() before
+    # the pseudo-input pass overwrites deq_cell's shared attributes (see
+    # Subtlety #4). None until the first forward() call.
+    self.last_batch_iters:    int|None = None
+    self.last_batch_residual: float|None = None
+
+  def _encode_via_deq(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    """Shared path for both real data and pseudo-inputs (see Subtlety #1)."""
+    h = self.encoder(x)
+    c = self.fc_cond(h)
+    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
+    return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)
+
+  def encode(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    return self._encode_via_deq(x)
+
+  def _pseudo_encoded(self) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    """
+    Encode all K pseudo-inputs through the same encoder+DEQ path as real
+    data (overrides VampPriorVAE._pseudo_encoded, which used a plain MLP
+    head). Called by the inherited vampprior_kl() -- this is also the call
+    that overwrites deq_cell's shared diagnostics (Subtlety #4).
+    """
+    zero_mask  = torch.zeros(self.n_pseudo, self.n_genes, device=self.pseudo_inputs.device)
+    pseudo_aug = torch.cat([self.pseudo_inputs, zero_mask], dim=1)
+    _, pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._encode_via_deq(pseudo_aug)
+    return pu_mu, pu_logvar
+
+  def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
+    """
+    Returns: (recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None) -- same 6-tuple contract
+    as every other model in this file.
+    Side-effects:
+      - self._vamp_kl set (read by epoch_vae's kl_override for this
+        isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE) branch).
+      - self.last_batch_iters / last_batch_residual snapshotted for the
+        *real batch* solve specifically (see Subtlety #4) -- read these
+        instead of self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters after this returns.
+    """
+    _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
+    # Snapshot now: vampprior_kl() below re-runs deq_cell on pseudo-inputs
+    # and would otherwise overwrite these.
+    self.last_batch_iters    = self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters
+    self.last_batch_residual = self.deq_cell.last_forward_residual
+
+    z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
+    recon = self.decode(z)
+    self._vamp_kl = self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)
+    return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
+
 
 def epoch_vae(model, loader, opt=None, mask_fraction=0.1, beta=1.0, gamma=0.2, min_free_bits=0.05, lambda_entropy=0.1):
   total_loss, total_err, grad_norm, recon_loss, kl_loss = 0.,0.,0.,0.,0.

# Investigation Report

## 1. `masked_loss` and `get_random_mask` (both defined in `synthetic_data.py`, re-exported/used by `models.py` via `from synthetic_data import masked_loss, get_random_mask, _random_fill`)

**`get_random_mask`** (`synthetic_data.py:191-204`):
```python
def get_random_mask(x:torch.Tensor, masked_fraction:float) -> torch.Tensor:
  type1_mask = torch.isnan(x)                                   # genuinely missing (dropout)
  type2_mask = torch.rand(x.shape) < masked_fraction
  type2_mask = torch.logical_and( type2_mask, ~type1_mask )     # deliberately masked, only on observed cells
  mask  = type2_mask | type1_mask                               # union: everything the model can't see
  x_masked = _random_fill(x, mask)                              # per-gene N(mean,std) fill at masked positions
  x_masked = x_masked + torch.randn_like(x_masked) * 0.05 * (~mask).float()  # small noise on observed-only positions
  return x_masked, mask, type2_mask
```
So there are two disjoint mask categories: **type1** (real, MCAR-missing values — loss can't be computed there because ground truth is unknown) and **type2** (deliberately held-out during training/eval — ground truth known, so loss *can* be computed there, used to score imputation quality).

**`masked_loss`** (`synthetic_data.py:222-294`):
```python
def masked_loss(x, pred, mask, mu, logvar, gate_probs=None, soft_weights=None,
                 mask_type2=None, beta=1.0, gamma=0.2, free_bits=0.1,
                 lambda_entropy=0.2, kl_override=None):
  _mask = ~mask
  sq_err = (pred - x).pow(2)
  recon_loss = (sq_err * _mask).sum() / _mask.sum().clamp(min=1)      # MSE on genuinely observed entries

  if mask_type2 is not None:
    recon_loss_type2 = (sq_err * mask_type2.float()).sum() / mask_type2.sum().clamp(min=1)
    recon_loss += recon_loss_type2 * gamma                             # gamma weights the type-2 (held-out) recon term
  else:
    recon_loss_type2 = torch.tensor(0.0)

  # KL:  -½ Σ (1 + log σ² - μ² - σ²)
  if kl_override is not None:
    # Pre-computed KL (e.g. VampPrior); free_bits clamping is skipped because
    # the VampPrior KL is a difference of log-densities and does not decompose
    # per dimension.
    kl_loss = kl_override

  elif mu.ndim==2:
    kl_per_dim = -0.5 * (1 + logvar - mu.pow(2) - logvar.exp())        # (B, latent_dim)
    kl_loss    = kl_per_dim.clamp(min=free_bits).sum(dim=1).mean()     # standard-Gaussian-prior KL with free-bits floor

  elif mu.ndim==3:
    # MoVE: weighted KL over mixture components
    kl_per_dim  = -0.5 * (1 + logvar - mu.pow(2) - logvar.exp())       # (K,B,D)
    kl_per_comp = kl_per_dim.clamp(min=free_bits).sum(dim=-1)
    kl_loss     = (kl_per_comp.T * soft_weights).sum(dim=0).mean()

  else:
    assert(False)

  if gate_probs is not None:
    avg_gate = gate_probs.mean(dim=0)
    gating_entropy_loss = (avg_gate * avg_gate.log()).sum()
  else:
    gating_entropy_loss = torch.tensor(0.0)

  loss = recon_loss + beta * kl_loss + lambda_entropy * gating_entropy_loss
  return loss, recon_loss, recon_loss_type2, kl_loss
```

Summary of the parameters:
- **`beta`**: weights the whole KL term against reconstruction (`loss = recon + beta*kl + ...`) — standard beta-VAE trade-off.
- **`gamma`**: weights the type-2 (deliberately-masked, ground-truth-known) reconstruction term *additively into* `recon_loss` — i.e. it's a multiplier on how much the held-out-imputation error counts relative to the ordinary observed-value MSE, not a separate additive loss channel.
- **`free_bits`**: a per-dimension (standard case) or per-component (MoVE case) floor via `.clamp(min=free_bits)` applied to the analytic Gaussian-KL-per-dim *before* summing — this is the classic "free bits" trick to prevent posterior collapse. It is **only used in the analytic closed-form KL branches** (`mu.ndim==2` and `mu.ndim==3`).
- **`kl_override`**: when provided (this is how `VampPriorVAE` supplies its KL — see `model._vamp_kl` in `models.py`), it **completely bypasses** the analytic Gaussian formula and free-bits clamping. The comment explains why: VampPrior's KL is `E_q[log q(z|x)] - E_q[log p_vamp(z)]`, a Monte-Carlo estimate of a difference of log-densities against a mixture prior, which doesn't decompose per-dimension, so free-bits clamping (which operates per-dim) isn't applicable/possible there.

Call site confirming how `kl_override` is set (`models.py` `epoch_vae`, ~line 734):
```python
kl_override = model._vamp_kl if isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE) else None
...
loss, recon_loss, recon_loss_type2, kl_loss = loss_func(
    x, pred, mask, mu, logvar, gate_probs, soft_weights,
    beta=beta, gamma=gamma, ... kl_override=kl_override, ...)
```
`model._vamp_kl` is set inside `VampPriorVAE.forward()` from `self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)`.

**Important asymmetry for the model-comparison question**: `free_bits` only regularizes the standard/MoVE Gaussian-KL path. VampPriorVAE's KL never receives the free-bits floor — its collapse-avoidance instead comes structurally from having a mixture-of-Gaussians prior (aggregate posterior can match a multimodal prior without individual-dimension collapse), which is a meaningfully different regularization mechanism than free-bits when comparing KL magnitudes/behavior across model types.

---

## 2. `make_synthetic_data5` and cluster/class generation (`synthetic_data.py`)

**`random_points_on_hypersphere`** (lines 297-383) — generates the cell-state manifold coordinates and *does* produce discrete cluster labels:
```python
def random_points_on_hypersphere(
    num_points: int, K: int, N: int,
    concentration: float = 0.0,
    n_clusters:    int   = 1,
    centre:        bool  = True,
    device: str = 'cpu',
) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
  ...
  # 1. Sample n_clusters centre directions on S^N
  centres = torch.randn(n_clusters, N + 1, device=device)
  centres = centres / torch.norm(centres, dim=-1, keepdim=True)

  # 2. Assign each point to a cluster uniformly at random
  labels = torch.randint(0, n_clusters, (num_points,), device=device)

  # 3. Sample points from vMF(centre[label], concentration)  (tangent-plane perturbation, higher concentration => tighter cluster)
  ...
  # 4-6: embed in K-dim space, random rotation, optional de-meaning
  return rotated, labels   # rotated: (num_points, K) cell-state coords; labels: (num_points,) int cluster id
```
So **class/cluster membership is a discrete label (`labels`, 0-indexed, uniform-random assignment among `n_clusters`)** that is used only to pick a von-Mises-Fisher center (`concentration` controls tightness) for that point's continuous position on the hypersphere. When `concentration ~ 0` the function even warns that cluster identity has *no* effect on the distribution (points are drawn uniformly regardless of label) — clusters only matter when `concentration > 0`.

**`make_synthetic_data5`** (lines 507-645) signature:
```python
def make_synthetic_data5(
    cell_state:     torch.Tensor,   # (n_cells, n_gene_groups) manifold coords, e.g. from random_points_on_hypersphere
    n_genes:        int = 200,
    corr_scale:     float = 1.0,
    noise_scale:    float = 0.001,
    n_gene_groups:  int = 32,           # must match groups_def
    means: torch.Tensor|None = None,
    stds:  torch.Tensor|None = None,
    allow_negative: bool = False,
    missing_rate:   float = 0.1,
    seed: int = 42,
    device: str|None = None,
    groups_def: dict = groups_def,      # 3-level hierarchical gene-group definition
    influence_gamma: tuple[float,float] = (10.0,10.0),
    latent_clamp: float = 15.0,
) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, np.ndarray]:
```
Key logic: genes are randomly (stochastically, per-gene) assigned into a hierarchical set of "gene groups" via `_draw_group_membership(groups_def)` producing a sparse binary `group_membership` matrix `(n_genes, n_gene_groups)`. The continuous `cell_state` (shape `(n_cells, n_gene_groups)`) is projected through this sparse membership matrix (`group_membership @ cell_state.T`), scaled by per-(gene,cell) Gamma-distributed influence weights and Gaussian noise, then mapped through a Negative-Binomial count model and `log1p`-transformed (with MCAR dropout as NaN), matching `make_synthetic_data4`'s output convention.

**`make_synthetic_data5` itself never takes `n_clusters`, `n_types`, or a `labels` argument** — it only consumes the *continuous* `cell_state` tensor. **It does not return any class/type/cluster labels** — its return is `(X, group_membership_np)`, i.e. gene→group structure, not cell→type structure.

**Conclusion for point 2**: In this codebase, "class"/"cell type" as a discrete label only exists as the `labels` tensor returned by `random_points_on_hypersphere` (driven by `n_clusters`/`concentration`), and it is *not* threaded through into `make_synthetic_data5` at all — the actual gene-expression generator (`make_synthetic_data5`) treats every cell's continuous `cell_state` as the sole driver of expression, so no discrete class attribute exists in its output. ## 3. `_effective_K` diagnostic in `VampPriorVAE` (`models.py:509-546`, inside `vampprior_kl`)

Exact code (lines 535-542):
```python
# effective K (diagnostic)
# range: 1.0 - n_pseudo
with torch.no_grad():
  log_resp = log_p_k - torch.logsumexp( log_p_k, dim=1, keepdim=True ) # (B, K)
  resp = log_resp.exp()
  # effective K = exp(mean_batch[ entropy(resp)])
  ent = -(resp * (resp + 1e-20).log()).sum(dim=1)  # (B,)
  self._effective_K = torch.exp(ent.mean())
```
Confirmed: it computes the per-sample "responsibility" distribution over the `n_pseudo` VampPrior pseudo-inputs (`resp`, a softmax-normalized `log_p_k`, i.e. how much each pseudo-input's Gaussian component contributes to that sample's mixture-prior density), takes the Shannon entropy of that responsibility distribution per sample, averages over the batch, and exponentiates. This is the standard "perplexity"/effective-number-of-components diagnostic: it ranges from 1 (only one pseudo-input effectively used/responsible on average) to `n_pseudo` (all pseudo-inputs contribute equally/uniformly on average). It's stored as `model._effective_K` and consumed in `vae-test.py:220-223` (logged into `learning_graph_train`) and in `models.py:1236` (`eff_k = float(getattr(self.vae, '_effective_K', torch.tensor(1.0)))`) as a monitoring/diagnostic signal for how many of the VampPrior's pseudo-input mixture components are actually being used.

The key issue is that VampPriorVAE's KL override mechanism bypasses the free_bits floor entirely, making it fundamentally different from standard VAE regularization. When effective_K collapses toward 1 (meaning the pseudo-inputs converge to a single mode), the mixture prior becomes fragile and analytically unstable compared to a simple Gaussian with free-bits. Combined with the DEQ's tanh-bounded latent mapping that compresses z* into a narrow range like (-0.9, 0.9), this severely limits information capacity and makes posterior collapse more likely, especially in less common latent regions. This suggests that higher between-class error variance and lower overall correlation reflect partial mode collapse—the model reconstructs common/typical cells well (near the collapsed pseudo-input attractor) but fails on cells whose true class is underrepresented in the effective pseudo-input set, creating a pattern where majority classes are reconstructed well while minority classes drag down aggregate metrics. The shared DEQCell weight coupling between pseudo-input and real-batch encoding likely compounds this issue.

Pseudo-inputs are optimized in a fully-observed regime (no masking applied), while real training data always has some fraction masked (typically 5-10%), creating a distribution mismatch. Since DEQCell uses a highly nonlinear fixed-point structure, the shared weights must simultaneously satisfy two different implicit equations across these different input regimes—a much harder constraint than in a simple feedforward network, since the fixed point itself is a globally-averaged nonlinear function rather than a local computation.

This regime mismatch could amplify optimization difficulties and representation errors more severely than in a standard VampPriorVAE, which only shares linear projections after the encoder. The result is worse overall fit and uneven quality across classes, since some conditioning vectors end up closer to well-optimized regions of the function than others, driving up between-class variance. The convergence budget (max_iter=30, tol=1e-4) was tuned for Stage 1 alone, but Stage 2 doubles the computational demands.

Each training step now solves the DEQ twice—once for the real batch and once for the K=50 pseudo-inputs—which could require different iteration counts due to their different input statistics. If either regime runs under budget, the training signal degrades. This could also just be a straightforward case of combining two untuned experimental components performing worse than either alone, which is common. The real issue might be that beta, the KL weight, learning rate, and epoch budget all need joint re-tuning rather than reusing the individually optimized settings from each stage.
