For diagnostics, I should track effective_K over training to see if it's collapsing toward 1, examine DEQ convergence behavior (last_batch_iters and residuals, potentially grouped by class difficulty), check whether the latent mean is saturating near the coefficient bounds before amplification, and run direct ablations comparing DEQEncoderVAE and VampPriorVAE in isolation to isolate whether the regression comes from the encoder, prior, or their interaction. Good — this pattern (higher between-class error variance **and** lower overall imputation correlation vs. simpler baselines) is diagnostically meaningful, and there are a few concrete, architecture-specific mechanisms in `DEQEncoderVampVAE` that could produce exactly this signature. I'll lay out the most likely ones, from the code I just re-checked.

## What the pattern likely means

Lower average correlation + higher *between-class* variance in error is not "uniformly worse" — it's the signature of **uneven capacity allocation**: the model fits some classes well and others poorly, and the poorly-fit classes drag down the average. That's consistent with a partial mode-collapse-like failure, not random noise or generic under-capacity (which would tend to hurt all classes more uniformly).

## Candidate mechanisms specific to this architecture

**1. VampPrior KL bypasses free-bits entirely, and its collapse-resistance depends on `_effective_K` actually staying high.**
`masked_loss` (synthetic_data.py) only applies the free-bits floor (`kl_per_dim.clamp(min=free_bits)`) on the analytic Gaussian-KL branches. When `kl_override` is set (the `VampPriorVAE`/`DEQEncoderVampVAE` path), that floor is skipped entirely — collapse-resistance relies solely on the mixture prior actually using multiple pseudo-inputs. If `_effective_K` (models.py:535-542, the entropy-based diagnostic already computed every forward pass) has collapsed toward 1, you've lost your only collapse-resistance mechanism, and behave more like a single flexible Gaussian — but with a less stable gradient path than a plain free-bits-regularized Gaussian KL. **First thing I'd check: plot `_effective_K` over training for this model vs. plain `VampPriorVAE`.** If it's collapsed, some classes' latent regions may only be weakly "anchored" by any pseudo-input, so their posteriors receive very little principled coverage.

**2. The DEQ fixed point is bounded, and that's a real bottleneck, not just cosmetic.**
`z* = coeff * tanh(W_zz @ z* + c)` means `z*` is confined to `(-coeff, coeff) ≈ (-0.9, 0.9)` per dimension *before* `fc_mu`/`fc_logvar` project it. Unlike a plain MLP head (unbounded `Linear(h)`), the DEQ's useful signal is squeezed through a saturating nonlinearity every single iteration to convergence — near the fixed point, `tanh` is often operating close to its saturation region for whichever dimensions are "assigned" a difference between classes, reducing gradient signal (`tanh'` → 0) exactly where the model needs to differentiate hard-to-separate classes. `fc_mu` can in principle rescale this back up, but that requires large, precisely-tuned weights that are harder to reach via the implicit-differentiation backward pass than via ordinary backprop through a deep MLP. This would show up as: common/central classes (near the "easy" region of the tanh) are handled fine, outlier/edge classes (needing larger separations) are compressed together, imputed poorly, and inconsistently — i.e. exactly higher between-class variance.

**3. The same `DEQCell` weights must jointly satisfy two different input regimes.**
Real batches always carry some masked fraction (`mask_fraction≈0.05–0.1`) into `c`, while the pseudo-inputs are encoded with an all-observed mask (`zero_mask` in `_pseudo_encoded`) — a systematically different input distribution. In plain `VampPriorVAE` this coupling exists too, but it's mediated by a single shared *feed-forward* MLP, whose output for masked vs. clean inputs is just two different points fed through a fixed function. Here, both regimes must additionally satisfy the *same self-consistent fixed-point equation*, which is a much tighter joint constraint on `W_zz`. If real-data conditioning vectors for particular classes land in a region where the fixed point is poorly conditioned relative to where pseudo-inputs happen to sit, those classes could get a worse-quality (or slower-converging) solve specifically.

**4. Convergence budget is shared/tighter than in Stage 1 alone.**
Every training step now runs `DEQCell` twice — once on the real batch, once on the 50 pseudo-inputs — with the same `max_iter=30`/`tol=1e-4` inherited unchanged from `_make_deq_encoder_vae`. If particular classes are systematically "harder" to encode (e.g., correspond to input statistics further from what the pseudo-inputs have converged well for), you'd expect them to need more iterations; if they're truncating at `max_iter` without reaching `tol`, their `z*` is a lower-quality, less-converged solution — directly increasing both the error and its class-conditional variance. **This is directly checkable**: log `last_batch_iters`/`last_batch_residual` (already exposed) per cell, grouped by class, and see whether the poorly-imputed classes correlate with higher iteration counts / larger residuals (non-convergence) rather than just "landing far from the manifold."

## Is it "just" a tuning problem?

Likely partly yes, but I wouldn't call it *only* a tuning problem. The hyperparameters (`beta`, `coeff=0.9`, `max_iter=30`, `n_pseudo=50`) were each carried over from the individually-tuned `_make_deq_encoder_vae`/`_make_vamp_vae` defaults, not jointly re-tuned for the composition — so under-tuning is a real, likely-dominant contributor. But mechanisms #2 and #3 above are structural interactions between the two components (tanh-bounded fixed point + shared weights across two input regimes) that plain hyperparameter tuning (e.g. just adjusting `beta`) won't fully fix — they may need architectural tweaks (e.g., un-shared conditioning paths for pseudo-inputs vs. real data, or a less aggressive `coeff`/larger `max_iter` to reduce saturation pressure).

## Suggested diagnostics to pin down which mechanism dominates

1. Plot `_effective_K` for `DEQEncoderVampVAE` vs. `VampPriorVAE` over training — collapse check.
2. Log `last_batch_iters`/`last_batch_residual` per cell, correlate with per-class error — convergence check.
3. Compare the per-class error variance for `DEQEncoderVAE` alone (Gaussian prior) and `VampPriorVAE` alone against `DEQEncoderVampVAE` — this tells you whether the regression is coming from the DEQ side, the Vamp side, or specifically their interaction (which of your "simpler models" have you actually compared against — do you have that 3-way breakdown, or just an aggregate "simpler models" baseline?).
4. Check the empirical range/std of `z*` pre-`fc_mu` vs. post — is `fc_mu`'s output actually reaching a comparable dynamic range to `VampPriorVAE`'s unbounded head, or is it under-scaled (saturation-bottleneck check)?

Do you already have per-class error logged in a way that lets you check #2/#3 quickly, or would it help if I sketch out how to add that instrumentation (still without executing anything, just planning it) before we decide on any actual code changes?

I'll also add a utility helper for computing the variance across classes from the per-cell errors and labels.

Now I'm implementing the core functions: a per-cell masked error calculator that computes mean squared imputation error over held-out genes for each cell, returning NaN where no genes were masked...

Writing diagnostic collection... I'm checking whether to pull the DEQ metrics from the model's stored snapshots or directly from the deq_cell attributes, then handling the VampPrior case to extract effective K values.

Now I need to make sure DEQEncoderVampVAE captures the per-sample residuals and z_star values before the pseudo-input pass overwrites them, so I'm planning to snapshot those in the forward method.

Writing forward method... Writing utility function...

I'm thinking about where to place these diagnostic functions in the codebase—right after the `impute()` function in models.py makes sense, before the model factories section. Now I'm considering whether we should thread class labels through the data generation pipeline itself.

The user already has a way to group by class based on their message, so rewiring the data generation could break their existing custom setup that I don't have full visibility into. I'll stick to what was explicitly requested: adding instrumentation on the model side to check effective K, iteration and residual convergence, and mu range saturation. That's the core ask, and I should focus there rather than making assumptions about their data pipeline.

For checking mu saturation, since `mu` is already returned by the forward pass, users can inspect it directly—I just need to store `last_z_star` (the pre-projection latent) so they can compare the spread and ratio between z_star and mu to see if the fc_mu layer is over or under-amplifying. Now I'll implement the changes: updating `_fixed_point_iterate` to return per-sample residuals, modifying both call sites to handle that return value, and adding the new tracking fields to DEQCell.

Adding new attributes and docstrings...

Let's implement instrumentation in three places: (1) per-sample residual/iteration tracking inside `DEQCell` (currently the fixed-point solver only tracks a single whole-batch scalar residual, so individual "hard" cells can't be identified — this is a real gap for the per-class analysis), (2) per-sample `_effective_K` in `VampPriorVAE`, and (3) a few small utility functions to build the per-cell/per-class table.

145: def _fixed_point_iterate(f, z0:torch.Tensor, max_iter:int=30, tol:float=1e-4):
146:   """Repeated substitution: iterate z <- f(z) until convergence.
147: 
148:   Minimal local copy of the same helper used by DEQBlock in
149:   normalizing_flow_demo.py (not imported from there because that module
150:   runs a full training demo as a side effect of import). Valid whenever f
151:   is a contraction (Lipschitz constant < 1), which DEQCell guarantees by
152:   construction (spectral-normalized weight * coeff < 1, composed with a
153:   1-Lipschitz tanh).
154: 
155:   Returns (z, n_iter, final_rel_change) so callers can check whether the
156:   solve actually converged within budget.
157:   """
158:   z = z0
159:   n_iter = max_iter
160:   rel_change = float("nan")
161:   for i in range(max_iter):
162:     z_next = f(z)
163:     rel_change = ((z_next - z).norm() / (z.norm() + 1e-6)).item()
164:     z = z_next
165:     if rel_change < tol:
166:       n_iter = i + 1
167:       break
168:   return z, n_iter, rel_change
169: 
170: 
171: class DEQCell(nn.Module):
172:   """
173:   Weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence: z* = coeff * tanh(W_zz @ z* + c),
174:   solved to its fixed point for a given conditioning vector c.
175: 
176:   This is the "DEQ as encoder" building block: instead of a fixed stack of
177:   N distinct MLP layers, a single shared transition is applied until it
178:   converges to self-consistency with the conditioning input c (derived
179:   from the observed/masked gene expression). This gives:
180:     - parameter efficiency: one shared (dim x dim) weight instead of N
181:       separate hidden-layer weights, relevant for the p >> N regime.
182:     - variable test-time compute: harder/more-incomplete rows can be run
183:       for more iterations without retraining or changing model capacity.
184: 
185:   Unlike DEQBlock in normalizing_flow_demo.py, this cell does not need to
186:   be invertible and never computes a Jacobian log-determinant (it isn't
187:   part of a bijective flow) -- it is only ever used as an encoder, which
188:   makes it considerably cheaper than DEQFlow's DEQBlock.
189: 
190:   Forward/backward follow the standard DEQ pattern (Bai, Kolter & Koltun,
191:   2019): solve for z* under no_grad, re-apply the transition once more
192:   with grad enabled to attach a shallow graph, then replace the
193:   backward-pass gradient with the solution of the adjoint fixed-point
194:   equation via a backward hook -- avoiding backprop through the unrolled
195:   solver entirely.
196:   """
197: 
198:   def __init__(self, dim:int, coeff:float=0.9, max_iter:int=30, tol:float=1e-4,
199:                n_power_iterations:int=5):
200:     super().__init__()
201:     self.coeff = coeff
202:     self.W_zz = nn.utils.spectral_norm(
203:         nn.Linear(dim, dim, bias=False), n_power_iterations=n_power_iterations)
204:     self.max_iter = max_iter
205:     self.tol = tol
206: 
207:     # Diagnostics populated on every forward() call, for inspection only.
208:     self.last_forward_iters:       int|None = None
209:     self.last_forward_residual:  float|None = None
210:     self.last_backward_iters:      int|None = None
211:     self.last_backward_residual: float|None = None
212: 
213:   def _f(self, z:torch.Tensor, c:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
214:     """
215:     Note: Lipshitz <= self.coeff < 1
216:     This guarantees z*=f(z*,c) has a unique solution (Banach fixed-point theorem)
217:     """
218:     return self.coeff * torch.tanh(self.W_zz(z) + c)
219: 
220:   def forward(self, c:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
221:     """c: (B, dim) conditioning vector -> z*: (B, dim) fixed point."""
222:     z0 = torch.zeros_like(c)
223:     with torch.no_grad():
224:       z_star, n_iter, residual = _fixed_point_iterate(
225:           lambda z: self._f(z, c), z0, self.max_iter, self.tol)
226:     self.last_forward_iters = n_iter
227:     self.last_forward_residual = residual
228: 
229:     z = self._f(z_star, c)  # value ~= z_star, but now differentiable
230: 
231:     if z.requires_grad:
232:       z0d = z.detach().requires_grad_()
233:       f0 = self._f(z0d, c)
234: 
235:       def backward_hook(grad):
236:         v, n_iter_bwd, residual_bwd = _fixed_point_iterate(
237:             lambda v: torch.autograd.grad(f0, z0d, grad_outputs=v, retain_graph=True)[0] + grad,
238:             grad, self.max_iter, self.tol,
239:         )
240:         self.last_backward_iters = n_iter_bwd
241:         self.last_backward_residual = residual_bwd
242:         return v
243: 
244:       z.register_hook(backward_hook)
245: 
246:     return z
247: 
248: 
249: class DEQEncoderVAE(VAEBase):
250:   """
251:   VAE with an implicit-depth (DEQ) encoder.
252: 
253:   Architecture:
254:     x_concat = [x_masked ; mask]            -- identical input every other
255:                                                 model in this file receives
256:     h = build_encoder(x_concat)             -- shared MLP feature extractor
257:     c = Linear(h)                           -- conditioning vector (latent_dim)
258:     z* = DEQCell(z -> coeff*tanh(W_zz@z + c))  -- weight-tied fixed point
259:     mu, logvar = Linear(z*), Linear(z*)
260:     z  = reparameterize(mu, logvar)
261:     recon = build_decoder(z)
262: 
263:   The prior is the standard N(0, I) and the decoder is the standard MLP
264:   decoder -- only the encoder's recognition network is replaced by a
265:   weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence instead of a fixed stack of MLP
266:   layers. This isolates the "DEQ as amortized encoder" role for direct,
267:   single-variable comparison against GeneExpressionVAE at matched
268:   encoder/decoder capacity.
269: 
270:   Note: like every other model in this file, the encoder receives
271:   randomly-filled values for missing entries (per-gene N(mean, std) noise,
272:   see get_random_mask / epoch_vae) plus a mask channel -- this class does
273:   not by itself remove that preprocessing step. What it adds is a
274:   weight-tied, variable-depth refinement of the latent given that input,
275:   with convergence diagnostics
276:   available via self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters / last_forward_residual.
277:   """
278: 
279:   def __init__(self,
280:                n_genes:int,
281:                latent_dim:int = 8,
282:                hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
283:                encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
284:                decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
285:                dropout:float = 0.05,
286:                coeff:float = 0.9,
287:                max_iter:int = 30,
288:                tol:float = 1e-4):
289: 
290:     super().__init__(n_genes,
291:                       latent_dim,
292:                       hidden_dims,
293:                       encoder_dims,
294:                       decoder_dims,
295:                       dropout)
296: 
297:     # Feature extractor (same shape convention as build_encoder elsewhere)
298:     self.encoder = build_encoder(n_genes, encoder_dims, dropout)
299:     self.fc_cond = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
300: 
301:     # Weight-tied implicit-depth recurrence
302:     self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff=coeff, max_iter=max_iter, tol=tol)
303: 
304:     # Latent heads (applied to the DEQ fixed point, not to h directly)
305:     self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
306:     self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
307: 
308:     # Build decoder
309:     self.decoder = build_decoder(n_genes, latent_dim, decoder_dims, dropout)
310: 
311:   def encode(self, x:torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
312:     h = self.encoder(x)
313:     c = self.fc_cond(h)
314:     z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
315:     return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)
316: 
317:   def decode(self, z:torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
318:     return self.decoder(z)
319: 
320:   def forward(self, x:torch.Tensor):
321:     _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
322:     z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
323:     recon = self.decode(z)
324:     return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
325: 
326:

334:     - Per-component decoders (component-specific generative model)
335:     - Gating network for component selection

Valid whenever f\n  is a contraction (Lipschitz constant < 1), which DEQCell guarantees by\n  construction (spectral-normalized weight * coeff < 1, composed with a\n  1-Lipschitz tanh).\n\n  Returns (z, n_iter, final_rel_change) so callers can check whether the\n  solve actually converged within budget.\n  \"\"\"\n  z = z0\n  n_iter = max_iter\n  rel_change = float(\"nan\")\n  for i in range(max_iter):\n    z_next = f(z)\n    rel_change = ((z_next - z).norm() / (z.norm() + 1e-6)).item()\n    z = z_next\n    if rel_change < tol:\n      n_iter = i + 1\n      break\n  return z, n_iter, rel_change",
  "newString": "def _fixed_point_iterate(f, z0:torch.Tensor, max_iter:int=30, tol:float=1e-4):\n  \"\"\"Repeated substitution: iterate z <- f(z) until convergence.\n\n  Minimal local copy of the same helper used by DEQBlock in\n  normalizing_flow_demo.py (not imported from there because that module\n  runs a full training demo as a side effect of import). Valid whenever f\n  is a contraction (Lipschitz constant < 1), which DEQCell guarantees by\n  construction (spectral-normalized weight * coeff < 1, composed with a\n  1-Lipschitz tanh).\n\n  Returns (z, n_iter, final_rel_change, final_rel_change_per_sample).\n\n  Instrumentation note: the stopping decision is made from a single\n  *whole-batch* scalar (`rel_change`, computed from the aggregate norm over\n  all rows) -- this matches the original behaviour exactly, so training\n  dynamics/results are unaffected. The extra `rel_change_per_sample` return\n  value (shape (B,), one scalar per row of z0) is a diagnostic computed\n  alongside it at no extra cost (same z/z_next already in hand), added so\n  callers can tell whether \"converged\" (by the aggregate check) actually\n  means every row converged, or just that most rows did while a few\n  particular rows (e.g. ones from an under-represented class) were still\n  changing a lot when the loop stopped. A single aggregate scalar cannot\n  distinguish those two cases; rel_change_per_sample can.\n  \"\"\"\n  z = z0\n  n_iter = max_iter\n  rel_change = float(\"nan\")\n  rel_change_per_sample = torch.full(\n      (z0.shape[0],), float(\"nan\"), device=z0.device, dtype=z0.dtype)\n  for i in range(max_iter):\n    z_next = f(z)\n    diff = z_next - z\n    rel_change = (diff.norm() / (z.norm() + 1e-6)).item()\n    rel_change_per_sample = diff.flatten(1).norm(dim=1) / (z.flatten(1).norm(dim=1) + 1e-6)\n    z = z_next\n    if rel_change < tol:\n      n_iter = i + 1\n      break\n  return z, n_iter, rel_change, rel_change_per_sample"
}

What it adds is a\n  weight-tied, variable-depth refinement of the latent given that input,\n  with convergence diagnostics\n  available via self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters / last_forward_residual.\n  \"\"\"",
  "newString": "  Note: like every other model in this file, the encoder receives\n  randomly-filled values for missing entries (per-gene N(mean, std) noise,\n  see get_random_mask / epoch_vae) plus a mask channel -- this class does\n  not by itself remove that preprocessing step. What it adds is a\n  weight-tied, variable-depth refinement of the latent given that input,\n  with convergence diagnostics available via self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters\n  / last_forward_residual (whole-batch), or\n  self.deq_cell.last_forward_residual_per_sample / last_z_star (per-cell --\n  see DEQCell's docstring) for e.g. correlating convergence quality with\n  per-class imputation error. Unlike DEQEncoderVampVAE, deq_cell here is\n  only ever called once per forward pass, so these attributes always\n  reflect the most recent real batch with no clobbering concern.\n  \"\"\""
}

551:   def vampprior_kl(self,
552:                    mu:     torch.Tensor,
553:                    logvar: torch.Tensor,
554:                    z:      torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
555:     """
556:     KL( q(z|x) || p_vamp(z) ) estimated with a single sample z ~ q(z|x).
557: 
558:     KL = E_q[log q(z|x)] - E_q[log p(z)]
559:        = E_q[log q(z|x)] - E_q[log (1/K) sum_k q(z|u_k)]
560: 
561:     mu, logvar, z : (B, D)
562:     returns scalar
563:     """
564:     # log q(z | x) : (B,)
565:     log_q = gaussian_log_prob(z, mu, logvar).sum(dim=-1)
566: 
567:     # Encode all pseudo-inputs once per forward pass
568:     pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._pseudo_encoded()  # (K, D)
569: 
570:     # Broadcast: z (B,1,D), pu_mu/pu_logvar (1,K,D)
571:     log_p_k = gaussian_log_prob(
572:         z.unsqueeze(1),           # (B, 1, D)
573:         pu_mu.unsqueeze(0),       # (1, K, D)
574:         pu_logvar.unsqueeze(0),   # (1, K, D)
575:     ).sum(dim=-1)                 # (B, K)
576: 
577:     # effective K (diagnostic)
578:     # range: 1.0 - n_pseudo
579:     with torch.no_grad():
580:       log_resp = log_p_k - torch.logsumexp( log_p_k, dim=1, keepdim=True ) # (B, K)
581:       resp = log_resp.exp()
582:       # effective K = exp(mean_batch[ entropy(resp)])
583:       ent = -(resp * (resp + 1e-20).log()).sum(dim=1)  # (B,)
584:       self._effective_K = torch.exp(ent.mean())
585: 
586:     log_p = torch.logsumexp(log_p_k, dim=1) - math.log(self.n_pseudo)  # (B,)
587: 
588:     return (log_q - log_p).mean()  # scalar
589:

  "newString": "    # effective K (diagnostic)\n    # range: 1.0 - n_pseudo\n    with torch.no_grad():\n      log_resp = log_p_k - torch.logsumexp( log_p_k, dim=1, keepdim=True ) # (B, K)\n      resp = log_resp.exp()\n      # effective K = exp(mean_batch[ entropy(resp)])\n      ent = -(resp * (resp + 1e-20).log()).sum(dim=1)  # (B,)\n      self._effective_K = torch.exp(ent.mean())\n      # Per-sample version of the same diagnostic: exp(entropy) for each\n      # individual cell's responsibility distribution over the K\n      # pseudo-inputs, rather than averaged over the batch first. A\n      # batch-level self._effective_K can look healthy (e.g. ~10 out of 50)\n      # while actually being an average of \"well-covered\" cells (many\n      # components effectively responsible, per-sample value close to K)\n      # and \"poorly-covered\" cells (collapsed onto ~1 component each) --\n      # the aggregate scalar can't distinguish uniform partial coverage from\n      # a bimodal mix. Grouping this per-sample value by class/label\n      # reveals whether specific classes are consistently under-covered by\n      # the mixture prior (a partial mode-collapse signature) rather than\n      # collapse being spread evenly across all cells.\n      self._effective_K_per_sample = torch.exp(ent).detach()  # (B,)"
}

  "newString": "    return recon, type2_mask.cpu()\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Per-cell / per-class diagnostics\n#\n# Built on top of impute() to support grouping per-cell imputation error (and\n# DEQ/VampPrior internal diagnostics) by an external class/cluster label, to\n# investigate questions like: \"is DEQEncoderVampVAE's higher variance in\n# imputation error across classes coming from uneven VampPrior mixture\n# coverage, uneven DEQ fixed-point convergence, or something else?\"\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\ndef per_cell_masked_error(x_raw: torch.Tensor,\n                           recon: torch.Tensor,\n                           type2_mask: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:\n    \"\"\"\n    Mean squared imputation error per cell, over that cell's type-2-masked\n    (deliberately held-out, ground-truth-known) genes only.\n\n    Args:\n        x_raw, recon, type2_mask : as returned by / passed to impute() --\n            (B, n_genes), aligned row-for-row. x_raw may contain NaN\n            (type-1/genuinely-missing positions); these are never counted\n            here because type2_mask is constructed to exclude them already\n            (see impute()).\n    Returns:\n        (B,) float tensor, CPU. NaN for any cell with zero masked genes in\n        this draw (can happen at very low mask fractions/small n_genes).\n    \"\"\"\n    x_raw  = x_raw.detach().cpu()\n    recon  = recon.detach().cpu()\n    type2_mask = type2_mask.detach().cpu()\n\n    sq_err = (recon - x_raw).pow(2)\n    n_masked = type2_mask.sum(dim=1).float()\n    per_cell = (sq_err * type2_mask.float()).sum(dim=1) / n_masked.clamp(min=1)\n    per_cell[n_masked == 0] = float('nan')\n    return per_cell\n\n\ndef collect_deq_diagnostics(model: nn.Module) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"\n    Snapshot the DEQ / VampPrior per-sample diagnostics currently held on\n    *model*, populated as a side effect of its most recent forward() call\n    (e.g. the call made inside impute()). Call this immediately after\n    impute()/model(...) and before any other forward pass on the same model,\n    since some of these attributes are overwritten on every call (see\n    DEQEncoderVampVAE's Subtlety #4).\n\n    Safe to call on any model type in this file -- entries are None if the\n    model doesn't have that diagnostic (e.g. GeneExpressionVAE has no\n    deq_cell; VampPriorVAE without a DEQ encoder has no deq_cell either).\n\n    Returns a dict with keys:\n        'deq_iters'               : int  or None -- whole-batch iteration count\n        'deq_residual'            : float or None -- whole-batch final residual\n        'deq_residual_per_sample' : (B,) tensor or None\n        'deq_z_star'              : (B, latent_dim) tensor or None -- pre-fc_mu\n                                     fixed point, for saturation/range checks\n        'effective_K'             : float or None -- VampPrior mixture usage,\n                                     whole-batch average\n        'effective_K_per_sample'  : (B,) tensor or None\n    \"\"\"\n    out = {\n        'deq_iters':               None,\n        'deq_residual':             None,\n        'deq_residual_per_sample': None,\n        'deq_z_star':               None,\n        'effective_K':              None,\n        'effective_K_per_sample':  None,\n    }\n\n    if isinstance(model, DEQEncoderVampVAE):\n        # Real-batch-specific snapshots taken in forward(), NOT deq_cell's\n        # raw attributes -- those get overwritten by the pseudo-input pass\n        # triggered inside vampprior_kl() (Subtlety #4).\n        out['deq_iters']               = model.last_batch_iters\n        out['deq_residual']            = model.last_batch_residual\n        out['deq_residual_per_sample'] = model.last_batch_residual_per_sample\n        out['deq_z_star']              = model.last_batch_z_star\n    else:\n        deq_cell = getattr(model, 'deq_cell', None)\n        if deq_cell is not None:\n            out['deq_iters']               = deq_cell.last_forward_iters\n            out['deq_residual']            = deq_cell.last_forward_residual\n            out['deq_residual_per_sample'] = deq_cell.last_forward_residual_per_sample\n            out['deq_z_star']              = deq_cell.last_z_star\n\n    if isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE):\n        effk = getattr(model, '_effective_K', None)\n        out['effective_K'] = float(effk) if effk is not None else None\n        out['effective_K_per_sample'] = getattr(model, '_effective_K_per_sample', None)\n\n    return out\n\n\ndef class_grouped_stats(values: torch.Tensor, labels) -> tuple[dict, float]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Group a per-cell diagnostic (e.g. per_cell_masked_error(...) output, or\n    collect_deq_diagnostics(...)['effective_K_per_sample']) by an external\n    class/cluster label and compute the between-class variance of the\n    per-class means -- i.e. the metric this whole investigation is about.\n\n    Args:\n        values : (N,) tensor or array-like, may contain NaN (excluded via\n                  nanmean within each class).\n        labels : (N,) array-like of class ids (int, str, whatever is\n                  hashable/sortable via np.unique), aligned row-for-row\n                  with values.\n    Returns:\n        (per_class_means, between_class_variance)\n        per_class_means         : {label: float} mean value within each class\n        between_class_variance  : float, variance across those per-class\n                                   means (population variance, ddof=0)\n    \"\"\"\n    values_np = values.detach().cpu().numpy() if torch.is_tensor(values) else np.asarray(values)\n    labels_np = np.asarray(labels)\n    assert values_np.shape[0] == labels_np.shape[0], \\\n        f\"values ({values_np.shape[0]}) and labels ({labels_np.shape[0]}) must align\"\n\n    per_class_means = {}\n    for lbl in np.unique(labels_np):\n        vals = values_np[labels_np == lbl]\n        per_class_means[lbl] = float(np.nanmean(vals)) if len(vals) else float('nan')\n\n    between_class_variance = float(np.var(list(per_class_means.values())))\n    return per_class_means, between_class_variance\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Model factories"
}

+class DEQEncoderVampVAE(VampPriorVAE):
+  """
+  Stage 2: VampPriorVAE with a DEQ (implicit-depth) encoder instead of a
+  plain MLP head.
+
+  Combines two previously-isolated changes:
+    - DEQEncoderVAE's weight-tied fixed-point recognition network (encoder
+      question / requirements b,c: missing-data handling, sample efficiency)
+    - VampPriorVAE's mixture-of-posteriors prior (prior question /
+      requirement a: multimodal, mode-collapse-resistant prior)
+
+  Subtlety #1 -- prior and posterior share one DEQCell:
+    Both the real batch and the K learnable pseudo-inputs are encoded
+    through the *same* DEQCell instance. This means the prior's mixture
+    components q(z|u_k) are fixed points of the identical implicit function
+    that defines the posterior q(z|x), just evaluated at different
+    conditioning vectors -- there is no separate "prior network" the way
+    there would be for, e.g., a learned normalizing-flow prior. This is a
+    deliberate design choice (keeps the "one weight-tied transition" DEQ
+    argument intact) but it does mean the two roles are coupled: any change
+    to W_zz that helps fit real data also reshapes every pseudo-input's
+    fixed point, and vice versa.
+
+  Subtlety #2 -- subclassing VampPriorVAE, not DEQEncoderVAE:
+    epoch_vae (models.py) dispatches the VampPrior KL override via
+    `isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE)`. Subclassing VampPriorVAE means that
+    check fires with zero changes to the shared training loop. The
+    DEQ-specific pieces (fc_cond, deq_cell) are grafted on top instead of
+    inherited from DEQEncoderVAE, since VampPriorVAE.__init__ already builds
+    everything else this class needs (encoder, decoder, pseudo_inputs,
+    vampprior_kl).
+
+  Subtlety #3 -- fc_mu/fc_logvar are re-declared, not reused:
+    VampPriorVAE.__init__ (via super().__init__ below) allocates
+    self.fc_mu / self.fc_logvar as Linear(enc_out_dim, latent_dim), sized to
+    consume the *raw encoder output* h directly (its own encode() applies
+    them to h). Here, mu/logvar must instead be read off the DEQ fixed point
+    z* (dim == latent_dim), so those two Linear layers are immediately
+    overwritten with Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim) versions after
+    super().__init__() returns. The two original Linear(enc_out_dim,
+    latent_dim) modules are allocated and then discarded (replaced before
+    any forward/optimizer step touches them) -- wasted construction cost
+    only, no effect on parameters actually trained or saved.
+
+  Subtlety #4 -- DEQCell diagnostics get overwritten by the pseudo-input pass:
+    DEQCell.forward() records convergence diagnostics (last_forward_iters /
+    last_forward_residual / last_forward_residual_per_sample / last_z_star)
+    as attributes on the DEQCell instance itself, updated on every call.
+    Within one forward() call here, deq_cell runs twice: once for the real
+    batch (inside encode()), and once more for the K pseudo-inputs (inside
+    vampprior_kl() -> _pseudo_encoded(), triggered right after). The second
+    call silently overwrites the first call's diagnostics, so reading
+    self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters (or any of the other last_forward_*
+    attributes) after forward() returns would reflect the pseudo-input
+    solve, not the real-batch solve we actually want to track (e.g.
+    iteration count / per-cell residual vs. mask fraction or class label --
+    the diagnostics DEQEncoderVAE/DEQCell were built to expose). Fixed by
+    snapshotting self.last_batch_* immediately after the real-batch
+    encode() call, before vampprior_kl() runs and clobbers deq_cell's
+    shared state. This is *not* an issue for self._effective_K /
+    self._effective_K_per_sample (VampPriorVAE.vampprior_kl) -- those are
+    computed exactly once per forward() call, from the already-encoded real
+    batch and pseudo-inputs together, so there's no second overwriting call.
+  """
+
+  def __init__(self,
+               n_genes:      int,
+               latent_dim:   int = 32,
+               n_pseudo:     int = 50,
+               hidden_dims:  list[int]|None = None,
+               encoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
+               decoder_dims: list[int]|None = None,
+               dropout:      float = 0.02,
+               coeff:        float = 0.9,
+               max_iter:     int = 30,
+               tol:          float = 1e-4,
+               pseudo_init_samples: torch.Tensor|None = None):
+
+    super().__init__(n_genes, latent_dim, n_pseudo,
+                      hidden_dims, encoder_dims, decoder_dims, dropout,
+                      pseudo_init_samples)
+    # VampPriorVAE.__init__ already built self.encoder, self.decoder,
+    # self.pseudo_inputs, and (see Subtlety #3) fc_mu/fc_logvar sized for
+    # the *wrong* input dim -- replace them here.
+    self.fc_mu     = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
+    self.fc_logvar = nn.Linear(latent_dim, latent_dim)
+
+    self.fc_cond  = nn.Linear(self.enc_out_dim, latent_dim)
+    self.deq_cell = DEQCell(latent_dim, coeff=coeff, max_iter=max_iter, tol=tol)
+
+    # Real-batch DEQ convergence diagnostics, snapshotted in forward() before
+    # the pseudo-input pass overwrites deq_cell's shared attributes (see
+    # Subtlety #4). None until the first forward() call.
+    self.last_batch_iters:             int|None = None
+    self.last_batch_residual:        float|None = None
+    self.last_batch_residual_per_sample: torch.Tensor|None = None
+    self.last_batch_z_star:              torch.Tensor|None = None
+
+  def _encode_via_deq(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    """Shared path for both real data and pseudo-inputs (see Subtlety #1)."""
+    h = self.encoder(x)
+    c = self.fc_cond(h)
+    z_star = self.deq_cell(c)
+    return h, self.fc_mu(z_star), self.fc_logvar(z_star)
+
+  def encode(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    return self._encode_via_deq(x)
+
+  def _pseudo_encoded(self) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
+    """
+    Encode all K pseudo-inputs through the same encoder+DEQ path as real
+    data (overrides VampPriorVAE._pseudo_encoded, which used a plain MLP
+    head). Called by the inherited vampprior_kl() -- this is also the call
+    that overwrites deq_cell's shared diagnostics (Subtlety #4).
+    """
+    zero_mask  = torch.zeros(self.n_pseudo, self.n_genes, device=self.pseudo_inputs.device)
+    pseudo_aug = torch.cat([self.pseudo_inputs, zero_mask], dim=1)
+    _, pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._encode_via_deq(pseudo_aug)
+    return pu_mu, pu_logvar
+
+  def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
+    """
+    Returns: (recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None) -- same 6-tuple contract
+    as every other model in this file.
+    Side-effects:
+      - self._vamp_kl set (read by epoch_vae's kl_override for this
+        isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE) branch).
+      - self._effective_K / self._effective_K_per_sample set by
+        vampprior_kl() (no clobbering concern, see Subtlety #4).
+      - self.last_batch_iters / last_batch_residual /
+        last_batch_residual_per_sample / last_batch_z_star snapshotted for
+        the *real batch* solve specifically (see Subtlety #4) -- read these
+        instead of self.deq_cell.last_forward_* after this returns.
+    """
+    _, mu, logvar = self.encode(x)
+    # Snapshot now: vampprior_kl() below re-runs deq_cell on pseudo-inputs
+    # and would otherwise overwrite these.
+    self.last_batch_iters               = self.deq_cell.last_forward_iters
+    self.last_batch_residual            = self.deq_cell.last_forward_residual
+    self.last_batch_residual_per_sample = self.deq_cell.last_forward_residual_per_sample
+    self.last_batch_z_star              = self.deq_cell.last_z_star
+
+    z = self.reparameterize(mu, logvar)
+    recon = self.decode(z)
+    self._vamp_kl = self.vampprior_kl(mu, logvar, z)
+    return recon, mu, logvar, None, None, None
+
 
 def epoch_vae(model, loader, opt=None, mask_fraction=0.1, beta=1.0, gamma=0.2, min_free_bits=0.05, lambda_entropy=0.1):
   total_loss, total_err, grad_norm, recon_loss, kl_loss = 0.,0.,0.,0.,0.
@@ -2081,6 +2283,133 @@ def impute(model, x_raw, frac, *, device=device):
     return recon, type2_mask.cpu()
 
 
+# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
+# Per-cell / per-class diagnostics
+#
+# Built on top of impute() to support grouping per-cell imputation error (and
+# DEQ/VampPrior internal diagnostics) by an external class/cluster label, to
+# investigate questions like: "is DEQEncoderVampVAE's higher variance in
+# imputation error across classes coming from uneven VampPrior mixture
+# coverage, uneven DEQ fixed-point convergence, or something else?"
+# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
+
+def per_cell_masked_error(x_raw: torch.Tensor,
+                           recon: torch.Tensor,
+                           type2_mask: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
+    """
+    Mean squared imputation error per cell, over that cell's type-2-masked
+    (deliberately held-out, ground-truth-known) genes only.
+
+    Args:
+        x_raw, recon, type2_mask : as returned by / passed to impute() --
+            (B, n_genes), aligned row-for-row. x_raw may contain NaN
+            (type-1/genuinely-missing positions); these are never counted
+            here because type2_mask is constructed to exclude them already
+            (see impute()).
+    Returns:
+        (B,) float tensor, CPU. NaN for any cell with zero masked genes in
+        this draw (can happen at very low mask fractions/small n_genes).
+    """
+    x_raw  = x_raw.detach().cpu()
+    recon  = recon.detach().cpu()
+    type2_mask = type2_mask.detach().cpu()
+
+    sq_err = (recon - x_raw).pow(2)
+    n_masked = type2_mask.sum(dim=1).float()
+    per_cell = (sq_err * type2_mask.float()).sum(dim=1) / n_masked.clamp(min=1)
+    per_cell[n_masked == 0] = float('nan')
+    return per_cell
+
+
+def collect_deq_diagnostics(model: nn.Module) -> dict:
+    """
+    Snapshot the DEQ / VampPrior per-sample diagnostics currently held on
+    *model*, populated as a side effect of its most recent forward() call
+    (e.g. the call made inside impute()). Call this immediately after
+    impute()/model(...) and before any other forward pass on the same model,
+    since some of these attributes are overwritten on every call (see
+    DEQEncoderVampVAE's Subtlety #4).
+
+    Safe to call on any model type in this file -- entries are None if the
+    model doesn't have that diagnostic (e.g. GeneExpressionVAE has no
+    deq_cell; VampPriorVAE without a DEQ encoder has no deq_cell either).
+
+    Returns a dict with keys:
+        'deq_iters'               : int  or None -- whole-batch iteration count
+        'deq_residual'            : float or None -- whole-batch final residual
+        'deq_residual_per_sample' : (B,) tensor or None
+        'deq_z_star'              : (B, latent_dim) tensor or None -- pre-fc_mu
+                                     fixed point, for saturation/range checks
+        'effective_K'             : float or None -- VampPrior mixture usage,
+                                     whole-batch average
+        'effective_K_per_sample'  : (B,) tensor or None
+    """
+    out = {
+        'deq_iters':               None,
+        'deq_residual':             None,
+        'deq_residual_per_sample': None,
+        'deq_z_star':               None,
+        'effective_K':              None,
+        'effective_K_per_sample':  None,
+    }
+
+    if isinstance(model, DEQEncoderVampVAE):
+        # Real-batch-specific snapshots taken in forward(), NOT deq_cell's
+        # raw attributes -- those get overwritten by the pseudo-input pass
+        # triggered inside vampprior_kl() (Subtlety #4).
+        out['deq_iters']               = model.last_batch_iters
+        out['deq_residual']            = model.last_batch_residual
+        out['deq_residual_per_sample'] = model.last_batch_residual_per_sample
+        out['deq_z_star']              = model.last_batch_z_star
+    else:
+        deq_cell = getattr(model, 'deq_cell', None)
+        if deq_cell is not None:
+            out['deq_iters']               = deq_cell.last_forward_iters
+            out['deq_residual']            = deq_cell.last_forward_residual
+            out['deq_residual_per_sample'] = deq_cell.last_forward_residual_per_sample
+            out['deq_z_star']              = deq_cell.last_z_star
+
+    if isinstance(model, VampPriorVAE):
+        effk = getattr(model, '_effective_K', None)
+        out['effective_K'] = float(effk) if effk is not None else None
+        out['effective_K_per_sample'] = getattr(model, '_effective_K_per_sample', None)
+
+    return out
+
+
+def class_grouped_stats(values: torch.Tensor, labels) -> tuple[dict, float]:
+    """
+    Group a per-cell diagnostic (e.g. per_cell_masked_error(...) output, or
+    collect_deq_diagnostics(...)['effective_K_per_sample']) by an external
+    class/cluster label and compute the between-class variance of the
+    per-class means -- i.e. the metric this whole investigation is about.
+
+    Args:
+        values : (N,) tensor or array-like, may contain NaN (excluded via
+                  nanmean within each class).
+        labels : (N,) array-like of class ids (int, str, whatever is
+                  hashable/sortable via np.unique), aligned row-for-row
+                  with values.
+    Returns:
+        (per_class_means, between_class_variance)
+        per_class_means         : {label: float} mean value within each class
+        between_class_variance  : float, variance across those per-class
+                                   means (population variance, ddof=0)
+    """
+    values_np = values.detach().cpu().numpy() if torch.is_tensor(values) else np.asarray(values)
+    labels_np = np.asarray(labels)
+    assert values_np.shape[0] == labels_np.shape[0], \
+        f"values ({values_np.shape[0]}) and labels ({labels_np.shape[0]}) must align"
+
+    per_class_means = {}
+    for lbl in np.unique(labels_np):
+        vals = values_np[labels_np == lbl]
+        per_class_means[lbl] = float(np.nanmean(vals)) if len(vals) else float('nan')
+
+    between_class_variance = float(np.var(list(per_class_means.values())))
+    return per_class_means, between_class_variance
+
+

I need to fix this by using `torch.where()` to conditionally select values based on the mask, or pre-emptively replace NaNs with zeros since they're masked out anyway. Rewriting per_cell_masked_error... Applying the fix now.

I need to fix a real bug I just introduced: `0 * NaN = NaN` in IEEE754, so multiplying `sq_err` (which is NaN at type-1-missing positions since `x_raw` has NaN there) by `type2_mask.float()` would silently contaminate every row that has *any* genuinely-missing gene, even though those positions are masked out. Let me fix `per_cell_masked_error`:

**Tool: edit**

**Input:**
```json
{
  "filePath": "/home/michael/src/vae-test/models.py",
  "oldString": "    x_raw  = x_raw.detach().cpu()\n    recon  = recon.detach().cpu()\n    type2_mask = type2_mask.detach().cpu()\n\n    sq_err = (recon - x_raw).pow(2)\n    n_masked = type2_mask.sum(dim=1).float()\n    per_cell = (sq_err * type2_mask.float()).sum(dim=1) / n_masked.clamp(min=1)\n    per_cell[n_masked == 0] = float('nan')\n    return per_cell",
  "newString": "    x_raw  = x_raw.detach().cpu()\n    recon  = recon.detach().cpu()\n    type2_mask = type2_mask.detach().cpu()\n\n    # NaN-safety: positions outside type2_mask can be NaN in x_raw (genuine\n    # type-1 missing values), and 0 * NaN == NaN in IEEE754 -- so a plain\n    # (sq_err * type2_mask.float()) would silently turn every row that has\n    # *any* type-1-missing gene into an all-NaN row, even though those\n    # positions are supposed to be excluded. nan_to_num + torch.where keeps\n    # the excluded positions from ever contributing/propagating NaN.\n    sq_err = (recon - x_raw.nan_to_num(0)).pow(2)\n    sq_err = torch.where(type2_mask, sq_err, torch.zeros_like(sq_err))\n    n_masked = type2_mask.sum(dim=1).float()\n    per_cell = sq_err.sum(dim=1) / n_masked.clamp(min=1)\n    per_cell[n_masked == 0] = float('nan')\n    return per_cell"
}

These new utilities are instrumentation for analyzing per-class variance, so the user will need to build the actual analysis loop in their evaluation script. ## Summary

I've added instrumentation across three areas in `models.py`, all additive (no changes to training behavior/results — verified via `ast.parse`, syntax OK; no runtime test since torch isn't installed here per `AGENTS.md`).

### 1. Per-sample DEQ convergence diagnostics (`_fixed_point_iterate`, `DEQCell`)

This closes a real gap: the solver's stopping decision — and previously its *only* reported residual — was a single whole-batch aggregate norm. A batch where a few "hard" rows are still changing a lot, mixed with many converged rows, can look identical to a batch of uniformly-slow rows once you only see the aggregate scalar. Now every `DEQCell.forward()` call also populates:
- `last_forward_residual_per_sample` — (B,) tensor, per-row residual at the same final iteration used for the aggregate check.
- `last_z_star` — (B, dim) tensor, the fixed point itself *before* `fc_mu`/`fc_logvar`, so you can check for tanh saturation (values piling up near ±`coeff`).

`DEQEncoderVAE` gets these for free. `DEQEncoderVampVAE` needed extra handling (models.py:702-753) since its shared `deq_cell` runs twice per forward pass (real batch, then pseudo-inputs) — the second call would silently clobber the first's diagnostics, so I added `last_batch_residual_per_sample`/`last_batch_z_star` snapshots taken right after the real-batch `encode()` call, before `vampprior_kl()` overwrites them.

### 2. Per-sample `_effective_K` (`VampPriorVAE.vampprior_kl`)

Added `_effective_K_per_sample` (models.py:585-599) — the existing `_effective_K` diagnostic averaged over the batch first, which can hide a bimodal split (some cells well-covered by many pseudo-inputs, others collapsed onto ~1) behind a healthy-looking mean. The per-sample version lets you check whether specific classes are the ones being under-covered.

### 3. Utilities for the actual per-class analysis (models.py:2286-2411)

- **`per_cell_masked_error(x_raw, recon, type2_mask)`** — mean squared imputation error per cell over its type-2-masked genes, built on `impute()`'s existing output convention. (Caught and fixed a NaN-propagation bug here: `0 * NaN == NaN` in IEEE754, so a naive masked-multiply would have silently turned every cell with *any* genuinely-missing gene into an all-NaN error.)
- **`collect_deq_diagnostics(model)`** — snapshots all the above (DEQ + VampPrior diagnostics) into one dict, safe to call on any model type in the file (returns `None`s for models without a `deq_cell`/`VampPriorVAE` base).
- **`class_grouped_stats(values, labels)`** — groups any per-cell tensor by an external label array and returns per-class means plus the between-class variance — the exact metric from your original question.

### How you'd wire this into your eval

```python
model.eval()
recon, type2_mask = impute(model, x_raw, frac=0.1, device=device)
err_per_cell = per_cell_masked_error(x_raw, recon, type2_mask)
diag = collect_deq_diagnostics(model)

err_means, err_between_var        = class_grouped_stats(err_per_cell, labels)
effK_means, effK_between_var      = class_grouped_stats(diag['effective_K_per_sample'], labels)
resid_means, resid_between_var    = class_grouped_stats(diag['deq_residual_per_sample'], labels)
```

Note: `labels` has to come from wherever you're already sourcing per-cell classes for your existing analysis — the committed `vae-test.py` data path (`selected_problem='standard4'`) currently hardcodes `types_train`/`types_test` to all-zeros and doesn't propagate `random_points_on_hypersphere`'s cluster labels through, so this instrumentation only becomes useful in combination with however you're already generating/attaching real class labels on your end.

## Interpretation

The most important number in this table isn't the between-class variance — it's `effK_means` itself: **it ranges from 1.0 to 2.11, out of a possible 50** (average ≈1.4). That means the VampPrior mixture has collapsed almost completely — for every class, essentially only 1–2 of the 50 pseudo-inputs are doing any work. This is the headline finding, independent of the original per-class-variance question: **Stage 2's whole reason for existing — a multimodal, mode-collapse-resistant prior — isn't actually happening in this trained instance.** The model is behaving like it has ~1 Gaussian prior component, except without the free-bits floor that protects the plain-Gaussian models from collapse (recall: `kl_override` bypasses free-bits entirely). That's a worse position than either baseline, not a neutral one.

**DEQ convergence is not the bottleneck.** Every `resid_means` value is tiny (1.9e-7 to 8.7e-5), several orders of magnitude below `tol=1e-4` — the fixed-point solver is converging cleanly for all 8 classes. This rules out my earlier hypothesis #4 (insufficient iteration budget for "harder" classes) — that's not what's happening here.

**But VampPrior collapse doesn't cleanly explain *which* classes have more error either.** Let me check the correlation:

**Tool: bash**

**Input:**

{
  "command": "python3 -c \"\nimport statistics\ndata = [\n (0.667, 1.65, 3.97e-05),\n (0.746, 1.00, 6.19e-06),\n (0.678, 1.56, 1.85e-06),\n (0.884, 1.60, 8.72e-05),\n (0.770, 1.14, 1.89e-07),\n (0.747, 2.11, 7.08e-06),\n (0.708, 1.00, 1.79e-06),\n (0.872, 1.03, 7.81e-06),\n]\nerr = [d[0] for d in data]\neffk = [d[1] for d in data]\nresid = [d[2] for d in data]\n\ndef corr(a,b):\n    ma,mb = statistics.mean(a), statistics.mean(b)\n    num = sum((x-ma)*(y-mb) for x,y in zip(a,b))\n    den = (sum((x-ma)**2 for x in a) * sum((y-mb)**2 for y in b))**0.5\n    return num/den\n\nprint('corr(err, effK)   =', round(corr(err,effk),3))\nprint('corr(err, resid)  =', round(corr(err,resid),3))\nprint('corr(effK, resid) =', round(corr(effk,resid),3))\nprint('effK std  =', round(statistics.pstdev(effk),3), ' mean=', round(statistics.mean(effk),3))\nprint('err std   =', round(statistics.pstdev(err),4),  ' mean=', round(statistics.mean(err),3))\n\"\n"
}

Looking at the correlation analysis, the weak negative correlation between error and effective stiffness makes intuitive sense—more effective components should reduce error—but it's not statistically strong with only eight samples. The moderate positive correlation between residual and error is more striking: even though the residuals are minuscule, their relative differences still show a meaningful relationship with error magnitude, suggesting that higher residuals correlate with higher errors.

The correlation between effective stiffness and residual is also moderate and positive, which is counterintuitive if I expected more components to make optimization harder. However, with only eight data points, I need to be cautious about statistical significance—none of these correlations are robust enough to claim real effects, since I'd need |r| > 0.7 to reach p < 0.05 at this sample size. The effective stiffness collapse is definitely real and severe, but it doesn't cleanly align with the per-class error ranking, and while the residual shows the strongest correlation of the three, I should present all of this with appropriate uncertainty given the small sample size. The effective component counts are indeed collapsed across all classes (ranging 1.0-2.11 with ~27% relative spread), so the real issue is that the mixture never becomes properly multimodal in the first place. Before diving deeper into per-class variance, I should fix the VampPrior collapse itself—that's the foundational problem that makes the Stage 2 comparison unfair. The concrete fix is to initialize the pseudo-inputs from actual diverse training samples rather than random noise, which should let the prior learn meaningful modes instead of collapsing to a single component. The recurrent structure in DEQ could amplify this collapse since shared weights between real and pseudo-input encoding might push pseudo-inputs toward whatever attractor dominates the real-batch gradients. I should explore adding a diversity regularizer that penalizes low pairwise similarity between pseudo-inputs, which is a known fix for VampPrior degeneracy. Another angle is β/KL annealing—if beta starts too high, it forces all posteriors to match the poorly-differentiated pseudo-inputs early on, locking in the collapse before optimization has a chance to separate them.

Given the user asked for interpretation, I should synthesize this into a clear answer about what's happening and ask whether they want me to validate this against a standard VampPriorVAE baseline or implement one of these fixes directly.

## Findings, quantified

| Comparison | r (n=8 classes) | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| err vs. effK | **-0.17** | weak — more mixture usage → slightly lower error, but far too weak to explain the ranking |
| err vs. resid | **+0.45** | moderate — but on residuals of magnitude 1e-7–1e-5, i.e. all "converged," so likely noise rather than a real convergence bottleneck |
| effK vs. resid | **+0.33** | weak/moderate, likely noise |

With only 8 classes, none of these correlations are strong enough to responsibly call causal (roughly |r|>0.7 needed for significance at n=8). So: **the per-class error variance you originally asked about is not well-explained by either diagnostic** — it's not primarily a DEQ-convergence problem (residuals are uniformly excellent), and while VampPrior mixture usage does track error in the expected direction, it's a weak effect, not the dominant one.

## The real finding here is different from what we were looking for

`effK_means` sitting at 1.0–2.11 out of a possible 50, for *every single class*, means the VampPrior has collapsed almost completely — the model is using essentially 1 effective pseudo-input regardless of class. That's worse than "not helping": since `kl_override` bypasses the free-bits floor entirely, a collapsed VampPrior gets *none* of free-bits' collapse protection and *none* of the multimodal-prior benefit either. Stage 2's whole premise (requirement (a), avoiding mode collapse) isn't being tested here — the mixture never became multimodal in the first place.

Given this, I'd treat the VampPrior collapse as the primary bug to fix, and treat the per-class variance question as currently unanswerable until it's fixed (you're not comparing "DEQ+multimodal-prior" vs. simpler models — you're comparing "DEQ+degenerate-1-component-prior" vs. them, which is a different, less interesting comparison).

The likely cause: `_make_deq_vamp_vae`/`_make_vamp_vae` both pass `pseudo_init_samples=None`, so `VampPriorVAE.__init__` initializes pseudo-inputs as `torch.randn(n_pseudo, n_genes) * 0.01` — all 50 pseudo-inputs start as nearly-identical tiny values clustered at the origin, giving them almost indistinguishable encoded posteriors from step 0. Nothing in training actively pushes them apart, so it's plausible they never separate.

_Thinking:_

So the gating entropy loss is computing negative entropy and adding it as a positive term to the overall loss—this means minimizing the loss actually pushes the model toward higher entropy in the gating distribution, which encourages more uniform gate activations rather than collapsing to a single expert.

Now I'm planning out the full answer: I'll diagnose why pseudo_init_samples alone didn't prevent collapse (it's an optimization dynamics problem, not initialization—there's nothing in the current loss penalizing the shared encoder from making all pseudo-inputs encode identically), then propose two complementary regularizers: a population-level responsibility-entropy term that mirrors the existing gating_entropy_loss using the same lambda_entropy hyperparameter, and a pairwise repulsion regularizer on the component means to prevent them from converging to identical solutions.

Good diagnostic result — and it actually confirms something important: **`pseudo_init_samples` alone can't fix this, because nothing in the current loss function penalizes collapse.** The KL term (`vampprior_kl`) only requires the *aggregate* mixture density `log_p = logsumexp(log_p_k) - log(K)` to be high at typical `z` values — that's exactly as satisfiable by all `K` pseudo-inputs converging to nearly-identical posteriors as by genuinely diverse ones (mathematically, a mixture of `K` identical Gaussians has the same density as `K=1`). Initializing pseudo-inputs at diverse real data points only affects the starting point; there's no gradient pressure keeping them apart afterward, so the shared encoder+DEQ (which is smooth almost by construction — that's the whole point of the contraction) just maps them back toward whatever attractor region the real batch is already pulling the network toward. Worse initialization variance actually shrinking (~1e-3) suggests they're now converging to a common region *more* consistently across training runs, not less.

## Design: two complementary regularizer terms

**1. Population-level responsibility-entropy regularizer (cheapest, matches existing codebase convention)**

`MoVEVAE` already has exactly this mechanism for its gating network (`synthetic_data.py:289,293`):
```python
avg_gate = gate_probs.mean(dim=0)
gating_entropy_loss = (avg_gate * avg_gate.log()).sum()   # = -H(avg_gate); minimized when uniform
loss = recon_loss + beta * kl_loss + lambda_entropy * gating_entropy_loss
```
`VampPriorVAE.vampprior_kl` already computes the per-sample responsibility distribution `resp` (currently `torch.no_grad()`, diagnostic-only). Mirroring the exact same pattern:

```python
# in vampprior_kl(), using log_p_k which is already differentiable (computed
# before the existing no_grad diagnostic block):
avg_resp = resp.mean(dim=0)  # (K,) -- population-average responsibility mass per pseudo-input, kept differentiable (not the no_grad copy used for _effective_K)
self._vamp_diversity_loss = (avg_resp * (avg_resp + 1e-20).log()).sum()  # same sign convention as gating_entropy_loss
```
Then in `epoch_vae`, after the existing `loss_func(...)` call:
```python
diversity_loss = getattr(model, '_vamp_diversity_loss', None)
if diversity_loss is not None:
    loss = loss + lambda_entropy * diversity_loss   # reuse the existing lambda_entropy hyperparameter -- same "encourage diverse component usage" semantics as MoVEVAE's gate
```
This requires computing `resp` outside the `no_grad` block (currently everything in that diagnostic section is wrapped in `torch.no_grad()`), so the existing block would need to be split: keep `_effective_K`/`_effective_K_per_sample` as pure no-grad diagnostics, but compute `resp`/`avg_resp`/`_vamp_diversity_loss` outside `no_grad()` so gradients flow back into the pseudo-input encodings (and, since they're shared, into the real-batch encoder/DEQ weights too — this is the mechanism that would actually push the pseudo-inputs apart during training).

**Caveat — this alone has a blind spot.** Entropy of `avg_resp` is maximized just as well by `K` *identical* pseudo-inputs (each getting the trivial 1/K share of responsibility for every sample, since a mixture of identical components has identical density from every component) as by `K` genuinely diverse ones. Responsibility-entropy regularization prevents *one component dominating everyone's responsibility*, but doesn't prevent *all components being redundant clones of each other*. That's a real, known gap — you could "fix" `effK` this way without actually getting a multimodal prior.

**2. Pairwise repulsion regularizer on pseudo-input encoded means (closes that gap)**

Directly penalize pseudo-inputs whose *encoded* posterior means (`pu_mu`, not the raw gene-expression parameters — since that's what actually determines whether the mixture components are distinct in the space that matters) are too close together:

```python
pu_mu, pu_logvar = self._pseudo_encoded()  # (K, D) -- already computed in vampprior_kl

pairwise_dist = torch.cdist(pu_mu, pu_mu, p=2)              # (K, K)
margin = 2.0  # tunable -- roughly the expected spread of the latent prior
off_diag_mask = ~torch.eye(self.n_pseudo, dtype=torch.bool, device=pu_mu.device)
repulsion = F.relu(margin - pairwise_dist[off_diag_mask]).pow(2).mean()
self._vamp_repulsion_loss = repulsion
```
Hinge-style (`relu(margin - dist)`) so it's bounded and only activates for pairs that are *too close* — once components are adequately spread out, this term contributes zero gradient, so it won't fight against the reconstruction/KL objective once diversity is achieved. Added to the loss with its own weight in `epoch_vae`:
```python
repulsion_loss = getattr(model, '_vamp_repulsion_loss', None)
if repulsion_loss is not None:
    loss = loss + lambda_vamp_repulsion * repulsion_loss   # new hyperparameter, e.g. default 0.1
```

## Recommendation

Implement both — they target genuinely different failure modes (responsibility concentration vs. component redundancy), and each has a blind spot the other closes. I'd also add one more diagnostic while we're in there: an **aggregate argmax histogram** (across the whole eval set, which pseudo-input index wins `argmax(resp)` most often) to distinguish "healthy per-cell specialization to genuinely different components" from "universal collapse to the same 1–2 components regardless of class" — right now `effK_between_var≈1e-3` (nearly identical low `effK` for every class) is consistent with true universal collapse, but per-sample entropy alone can't fully rule out healthy specialization either, since a perfectly-specialized mixture would also show `effK≈1` per cell.

I'll sketch a code approach without applying changes yet, walk through why simple reinitialization alone probably didn't solve the collapse problem, then lay out two complementary regularization strategies—one using entropy to encourage uniform gating and another using repulsion to spread activations across experts. Before diving into implementation, I want to clarify how you'd prefer to integrate these mechanisms and what hyperparameter ranges make sense for your setup.

## Why did pseudo_init_samples make it worse — briefly

This is expected, and it's diagnostic in itself: nothing in the current loss function actually penalizes collapse. `vampprior_kl`'s only requirement is that the *aggregate* mixture density `log_p = logsumexp(log_p_k) - log(K)` be high at typical `z` values — and mathematically, K identical (or near-identical) components achieve exactly the same aggregate density as K=1 would. Collapse is a perfectly valid, often *easier-to-reach* optimum for this objective; nothing fights against gradient descent finding it. Initializing pseudo-inputs at real data points gives them a better starting spread, but with no ongoing force keeping them apart, and with all `n_pseudo` inputs pushed through the *same* shared encoder/DEQ weights (Subtlety #1), they're free to re-converge — and did, slightly faster/further this time.

You need an explicit term in the loss that rewards diversity. Here's how I'd design it — **two complementary regularizers**, because they catch different failure modes:

## 1. Population-level responsibility-entropy regularizer

This mirrors a pattern **already in this codebase** — `MoVEVAE`'s `gating_entropy_loss` (synthetic_data.py:289-293) does exactly this for its gating network, reusing the existing `lambda_entropy` hyperparameter that's already threaded through `epoch_vae`/`vae-test.py`. I'd add the same pattern for VampPrior's pseudo-input responsibilities:

```python
# Inside VampPriorVAE.vampprior_kl(), alongside the existing log_p_k computation
# (which is already differentiable -- unlike the diagnostic block below it,
# this needs to keep its gradient):

log_resp = log_p_k - torch.logsumexp(log_p_k, dim=1, keepdim=True)  # (B, K)
resp = log_resp.exp()
avg_resp = resp.mean(dim=0)  # (K,) -- aggregate responsibility mass per component, across the whole batch

# Same sign convention as MoVEVAE.gating_entropy_loss: this is (avg_resp *
# log(avg_resp)).sum(), which ranges from -log(K) (avg_resp uniform, i.e.
# every pseudo-input gets roughly 1/K of the batch's responsibility mass)
# to 0 (avg_resp one-hot, i.e. one pseudo-input absorbs virtually the whole
# batch). Adding lambda_entropy * this term to the loss therefore pushes
# the optimizer toward uniform aggregate usage across pseudo-inputs.
self._vamp_diversity_loss = (avg_resp * (avg_resp + 1e-20).log()).sum()
```

Then in `epoch_vae`, after the existing `loss_func(...)` call:

```python
diversity_loss = getattr(model, '_vamp_diversity_loss', None)
if diversity_loss is not None:
    loss = loss + lambda_entropy * diversity_loss
```

This reuses `lambda_entropy` (already a per-run hyperparameter, e.g. `0.01` in `vae-test.py`) rather than introducing a new one — consistent with its existing meaning ("encourage diversity in a component-selection distribution").

**Important blind spot**: this only measures whether responsibility is *evenly distributed* across components — but if all K pseudo-inputs are literally identical parameters, `log_p_k` is identical across `k` for every `z`, so `resp` comes out perfectly uniform trivially, with zero actual diversity in what the components represent. Entropy regularization alone can be satisfied by "K indistinguishable clones," which doesn't give you a real multimodal prior — it just looks non-collapsed on this one metric while still being functionally collapsed.

## 2. Explicit geometric repulsion on pseudo-input encodings

This directly targets the loophole in #1 by forcing the encoded pseudo-input means apart in latent space, independent of how responsibility mass is distributed:

```python
# Also inside vampprior_kl(), using pu_mu (K, D) already computed via
# self._pseudo_encoded():

pairwise_sq_dist = torch.cdist(pu_mu, pu_mu, p=2).pow(2)  # (K, K)
K = pu_mu.shape[0]
# Push the diagonal (self-distance = 0) out of consideration so it never
# dominates the margin penalty below.
pairwise_sq_dist = pairwise_sq_dist + torch.eye(K, device=pu_mu.device) * 1e6

# Hinge-style repulsion: only pairs closer than `margin` incur any penalty,
# so components that are already well-separated get zero gradient from this
# term (keeps it stable -- doesn't push components to infinity).
margin = 2.0  # tunable; roughly the expected per-dimension latent scale
repulsion = F.relu(margin - pairwise_sq_dist.sqrt()).pow(2).mean()
self._vamp_repulsion_loss = repulsion
```

Added the same way in `epoch_vae`, with its own weight (separate from `lambda_entropy`, since this is a geometric constraint, not an entropy target):

```python
repulsion_loss = getattr(model, '_vamp_repulsion_loss', None)
if repulsion_loss is not None:
    loss = loss + lambda_vamp_repulsion * repulsion_loss   # new hyperparameter
```

## Why both, not just one

- Entropy regularizer (#1) alone → can be satisfied by identical/redundant components (no real diversity, just uniform "sharing" of a non-diverse mixture).
- Repulsion regularizer (#2) alone → forces components apart geometrically, but doesn't prevent one dominant component from still absorbing almost all responsibility while a few outlier components sit far away contributing negligible density (i.e., you could get well-separated but still near-unused far-flung pseudo-inputs).
- Together, they push toward the actual goal: components that are both spatially distinct *and* meaningfully shared in responsibility.

Both attach cleanly to `DEQEncoderVampVAE` automatically (inherits `vampprior_kl` unchanged from `VampPriorVAE`), and both are additive to `epoch_vae` (no signature changes to `masked_loss` needed, just extra terms added to the returned `loss` before `.backward()`).

## Questions before I implement

1. Want both regularizers, or start with just the entropy one (cheaper, reuses existing `lambda_entropy`) and add repulsion only if collapse persists?
2. For repulsion: is `margin=2.0` a reasonable guess given your `latent_dim=32` and the DEQ's `tanh`-bounded fixed point (recall `z*` is confined to `(-0.9, 0.9)` per dim before `fc_mu` — do you want the margin defined in raw `pu_mu` space, post-`fc_mu`, as I sketched, or would you rather compute it on `z*` pre-`fc_mu` where the scale is more constrained/known)?
3. Should these regularizers apply to `VampPriorVAE` too (plain, no DEQ), so you get an apples-to-apples comparison of whether fixing collapse helps both models equally, or is this purely for `DEQEncoderVampVAE` right now? (I'd lean toward both, given #1 from your control-check suggestion earlier — worth checking whether plain `VampPriorVAE` collapses too.)
